0,2026-10-02,"Manchester City have won all five of their Premier League games this season
So much has been said about Manchester City since it was revealed they had been found guilty of the majority of the 115 Premier League charges levelled at them.
On Tuesday, it was confirmed an independent commission found City guilty of all charges relating to breaches of the league's financial regulations between the 2009-10 and 2017-18 seasons. The club has repeatedly denied guilt. 
Their potential punishment has been debated, the future of the owners questioned and the feelings of the rest of the league - and wider football world - have been aired.
But so far there has been little focus on what it means for one crucial group.
Their current squad.
Without definitive clarity on what sanctions the Premier League will impose, pending the appeal City have lodged, there is uncertainty over their ability to retain players, renew contracts, and make transfers - and whether any of the team have release clauses.
Put simply, will players want to join? Will current players want to stay?
BBC Sport has spoken to a number of sources in an attempt to understand how the recent judgement will impact City's dealings in the transfer market and how existing players may approach their futures with the prospect of losing Premier League status hanging over the club.
Man City confirm appeal against guilty verdict
Manchester City vs The Premier League
Criminal investigation? Relegation or expulsion? The key Man City questions
The general train of thought is that any drop down the leagues for City will result in a mass exodus.
That players will, at the very least, consider their futures at Etihad Stadium in the event of demotion would be understandable.
The squad is brimming with international talent, many of whose profiles and personal sponsorship agreements benefit from plying their trade in the Premier League.
The reaction from current members of the City squad varies depending on who you speak to.
From the conversations BBC Sport have had with sources close to the first team, there is certainly a significant level of concern as to how they will be affected.
Given the scathing tone of the Premier League's statement on Tuesday, there is major scepticism from people close to the players that the forthcoming appeals will earn the club any reprieve.
But there are others who absolutely believe in City executives Khaldoon Al Mubarak and Ferran Soriano's vows to prove the club's innocence.
This video can not be played
Will there be a mass exodus at Man City?
For those players who decide they don't want to drop down the divisions, should it come to that, the process of leaving is unlikely to be straightforward.
Firstly there is the contract and the player registrations. City, regardless of each individual's view on whether they want to depart, have the right to demand transfer fees.
Given how much those players are worth in terms of wages and their general value in the market you'd imagine City are unlikely to accept being undercut.
That, as BBC Sport understands, the majority of players do not have relegation clauses in their contracts also plays into the club's hands.
But it is also understood that as a general rule the players' contracts are fully guaranteed, meaning they would not be required to take percentage pay cuts if they lose their top-flight status.
With one of the highest wage bills in the world, maintaining that level of salary expenditure, even for a club as financially capable as City, will become harder without Premier League-level income.
Parachute money could ease those concerns, although there are no guarantees that the club would be entitled to those payments if they are demoted.
With that in mind, City deciding to keep the squad completely intact should they move down the leagues appears unlikely.
Man City Q&A with Kieran Maguire & Dale Johnson
How amenable City are to selling players will be dependent on how bullish they are about it all.
Are they a distressed seller? Probably not. They still hold strong control of the situation regardless of how their players may feel.
It is unlikely the club will just simply roll over and let disillusioned players depart without suitable offers because, ultimately, they can ride the storm financially.
Players will have massive decisions to make too, not just in respect of leaving.
City are big payers, and other clubs may not be willing to meet similar wage demands.
""Some players want medals, all players want money,"" one agent said to BBC Sport.
In terms of potential contract renewals, the current uncertainty over what sanctions will be meted out provides a major element of risk.
Amending a contract retrospectively, say for example to include a relegation clause, generally cannot happen.
Any deals that include exit strategies for players if the club fall out of the Premier League would have to be negotiated as new contracts.
Players will likely want to wait for clarity on the sanctions before entering into contract discussions, adding a layer of difficulty for the club.
One way round persuading a player to renew would be to offer a significant financial incentive to re-sign to negate the risk of relegation.
But a number of agents who spoke to BBC Sport believe players will be advised to wait for clarity on the punishments before even considering opening talks.
Thankfully for City, only Mateo Kovacic, Matheus Nunes, Marcus Bettinelli and Geronimo Rulli have contracts expiring in the next two summers, so that issue isn't as urgent as it could have been.
City's potential challenges with player contracts comes with Fifa preparing to introduce new rules that state every deal signed on 1 January, 2027 or after must include a release clause.
Guardiola backing Man City after guilty verdicts
Punish Man City this season, say other club chiefs
HMRC urged to scrutinise tax implications of Man City case
Manchester City chief executive officer Ferran Soriano (left) and chairman Khaldoon Al Mubarak hold the Champions League trophy in 2023
The looming punishment certainly complicates City's plans in the transfer market.
""This isn't the players' failing, it's on the club. Potential new signings aren't even in the building and joining City without knowing what the punishment would be represents an undeniable risk, so any new contracts would have to have indemnities,"" said one leading agent.
It was acknowledged that writing eventualities into new registrations is very difficult, though City players do have release clauses in their deals should the club enter into administration.
Until a post-appeal verdict over sanctions is made, making new signings will certainly become more complicated, though it is still likely they will be able to attract big players as they still have the financial muscle to do so.
For example, one potential method of persuading a player to join would be to offer them a contract on big money with no pay decreases if the club are relegated, and then include a fixed fee release clause if City don't return to the top flight within a set period.
If sanctions ensure relegation and include further points deductions that stretch over multiple seasons, attracting top players will of course be harder.
City could make a wage package hard to resist for elite players, but it seems inconceivable that a footballer of that ilk would join with demotion a possibility as there are likely to be other top options elsewhere.
The same logic can be applied to the retention of their current squad.
There is almost certainly going to be interest in their top players from other elite clubs across Europe. It will be very difficult for City to keep that level of player in the coming months and years while the saga plays out.
So with all that said, particularly if relegation is confirmed, City will have to rebuild their squad to some extent, though the prospect of a transfer embargo as one of the sanctions would prevent them from making any signings at all.
Such a rebuild may include the promotion of some of the club's best academy players, who stand to benefit if the first-team squad disbands.
When Chelsea were placed under a transfer embargo in 2019, the likes of Mason Mount, Tammy Abraham, Fikayo Tomori, Billy Gilmour and Callum Hudson-Odoi all emerged as senior players.
And while City may look to rebuild with a profile of player they believe will be adept at being successful in the EFL, you will likely see a number of their younger players fast-tracked.
With that said, a number of clubs are already working on seeing if they can capitalise on the current uncertainty by luring City's best academy talent.

1,2026-10-02,"Tesla's EV business is still going strong.
Elon Musk's electric carmaker announced on Friday that it sold 486,532 vehicles in the third quarter, down 2% year over year.
The figures came in well above Wall Street's expectations. Tesla shares rose more than 2% in premarket trading on the news.
Analysts were expecting the company to deliver around 463,761 EVs, according to a consensus compiled by Bloomberg, while Tesla's own compilation of analyst forecasts predicted 462,000 deliveries.
Tesla posted record sales in Q3 last year as buyers rushed to take advantage of the $7,500 tax credit for new EVs, which expired in September 2025.
Since then, the US EV market has cratered. Sales of electric vehicle models in August were down 47% from 2025, per data from Cox Automotive, and a host of automakers have rolled back their EV plans and pulled electric models from the US market.
Tesla has ridden out the EV winter better than most. The Model Y maker has increased its share of the US market since the tax credit went away, and posted a mega sales beat in the second quarter.
The reward for that good news was Tesla's worst stock price drop in a year, with investors increasingly showing more interest in the company's robotics and autonomous vehicle endeavors.
Tesla began adding the Cybercab, a gold-clad robotaxi without pedals or a steering wheel, to its robotaxi fleet in September, and is gearing up to begin production of its Optimus humanoid robot before the end of the year.

The company's robotaxi network remains limited to six cities in Texas and Florida, lagging rivals Waymo and well behind the ambitious targets set out by Musk at the end of last year.
The relatively slow progress has weighed on Tesla's share price, which is down more than 20% since the start of the year. Investors have one eye on rumors of a potential merger with SpaceX, which has eclipsed Tesla's market cap since its record-breaking public listing in June.
Musk and other SpaceX executives have hinted that a merger could be on the cards, with the billionaire teasing a potential combination when asked about the relationship between the two companies in a September interview.
""With all this collaboration, on so many levels, who can imagine what what action one might take?"" Musk said.

2,2026-10-02,"Zack Polanski will address the Green Party's conference in Brighton on Friday
Zack Polanski is set to call for a three-year ""emergency brake"" on private rent increases when he opens the Green Party's conference later.
In his speech to party activists on Friday afternoon, the Green leader will seek to frame housing as a key issue of generational fairness.
He will urge the government to prevent private rents rising above whichever is lowest out of the consumer price index measure of inflation, wage growth or 2%, for three years.
According to the party, he will also argue pressure from the Greens has forced Labour into a change of political direction on some issues.
The Green Party of England and Wales is billing the gathering in Brighton as its biggest ever, after announcing earlier this year that its membership had tripled since Polanski's election as leader just over a year ago.
In his speech, Polanski is expected to say generations are having to live with their parents because of rent prices, from 19-year-olds commuting to university, 25-year-olds unable to find a job, to 35-year-olds moving back home with their partners. 
""For too long, landlords have been able to hike prices way beyond wages, leaving renters forced to meet the cost to keep a roof over their heads, cutting back and back and back on everything else,"" he is expected to say. 
A slowdown on private rent increases ""would give renters vital breathing space"" and give time for ministers to create a national Fair Rents Guarantee that keeps rents affordable for the future, he is expected to say.
Earlier this year, the government brought in the Renters' Rights Act under which landlords can only increase rent once a year to the property's open market rate.
Average UK monthly private rents increased by 3.5%, to Â£1,377, in the 12 months to January 2026, according to the Office for National Statistics.
The Act also means tenancies are rolling rather than fixed term, and makes it harder for landlords to evict tenants. 
But Polanski is expected to argue it does not go far enough in ending ""fear and uncertainty"" for the millions of private renters in the UK.
The Institute for Fiscal Studies has previously warned, external that rent controls - an issue the Greens have long campaigned for - would result in ""unintended consequences"" including demand exceeding supply in the rental market.
However, the party's co-deputy leader Rachel Millward told BBC Breakfast most landlords were ""super-earners"", who were making more money than other sectors and ""would be able to absorb a slight reduction in rent"".
The Green conference follows the party gaining hundreds of councillors earlier this year at England's local elections and its fifth MP, Hannah Spencer, in the Gorton and Denton by-election.
But there are signs its rise in national polls may have stalled in recent months, while the party also faces questions over how to react to Labour's change of leadership under Andy Burnham.
Since Burnham became prime minister, Labour has shifted its stance in a number of areas where it had been in danger of being outflanked politically by the Greens.
In an interview ahead of the conference, Millward said Labour was now ""rhetorically, on a much more similar ground"" to her party.
This includes Burnham's move towards electoral reform, where the Greens have long demanded a shake-up of the Westminster voting system, and opening the door to a much closer future relationship with the European Union.
Labour has also announced sanctions on Israeli West Bank settlements deemed illegal under international law, and the reopening of a scheme to resettle refugees. 
Speaking ahead of the conference, Polanski claimed those two policies were a result of the electoral pressure his party has applied to Labour.
The timing of the conference could, however, prove awkward for Polanski, who is standing for the Greens in next week's Holborn and St Pancras by-election triggered by Sir Keir Starmer's decision to leave Parliament.
Polanski said last week he was intending to come ""straight back"" to the central London seat after his leader's speech.
He has even urged the party's activists to leave the conference over the weekend to help him campaign.
Victory in the contest would give the Greens their first seat in the capital, but would require them to overturn a huge Labour majority from the last election in 2024. A full list of candidates can be found here.
Correction - an earlier version of this story incorrectly said Green Party MP Hannah Spencer won a by-election in Makerfield. She actually won the by-election in Gorton and Denton.

3,2026-10-02,"The Unpromotables: Germany's league champions who can't go up
Djamal Ziane is a three-time league champion â but he does not always feel like it.
This summer, for the second year in a row and for the third time in seven seasons, the Lokomotive Leipzig captain hoisted the trophy into the air having led his team to the title in the Regional League Northeast, the fourth tier of German football.
But the memory is tinged with frustration because, just as in 2025 and 2020, Lokomotive's league title did not lead to promotion.
While the other regional champions in the German fourth division were promoted to the nationwide 3. Liga, ""Lok"" faced a play-off against a representative of the Regional League Bavaria, Wurzburger Kickers. Ziane scored, but an injury-ravaged Leipzig lost 3-1 on aggregate.
""Euer System ist krank,"" read a simple protest banner displayed by the 2,000 travelling supporters â ""your system is sick"".
""It's the worst thing â injury aside â that you can experience as a footballer,"" Ziane tells BBC Sport.
""Catastrophe doesn't cut it. It doesn't make any sense. If they really wanted to change it, they could do tomorrow."" 
But it's not that simple.
A banner reading 'Your system is sick' was displayed at this year's promotion play-off
Germany's football pyramid has a bottleneck.
Beneath the Bundesliga, 2. Bundesliga and 3. Liga, the fourth tier is split into five regional divisions (North, West, Southwest, Northeast and Bavaria), each of which produces a league champion.
So far, so good. But with only four teams being relegated from the 3. Liga each season, the sums don't add up.
An unsatisfactory solution is currently in place: the champions in the West and Southwest go up automatically while a third automatic promotion spot is rotated among the other three leagues in a three-year cycle. The fourth spot is allocated via a play-off between the winners of the remaining two leagues.
One champion always misses out and, increasingly, it's the champion from the Regional League Northeast, covering the territory of the former eastern German Democratic Republic (GDR).
In addition to Lok Leipzig's triple tragedy, former serial GDR Oberliga winners Dynamo Berlin fell short in 2022 and Energie Cottbus drew the short straw in 2023. This season, there is only one eastern German club in the 3. Liga: Hansa Rostock.
The struggles of the regions' football clubs are, to some degree, a result of continuing socio-economic disparities between east and west, which were also reflected in the success of the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party in recent state elections.
Even in 2026, not one of the 40 leading companies listed on Germany's DAX stock exchange is based in the former east (outside Berlin). Salaries remain lower, unemployment remains higher and the population is older and shrinking as young people move west.
The reason that automatic promotion spots are handed to the champions in the West and Southwest is simply because there are more men's football teams registered in those jurisdictions - because more people live there. 
It is an argument that Ziane is tired of hearing. 
""In that case, you might as well give us the automatic promotion spot because we attract bigger crowds,"" he says.
Indeed, the average attendance in the Regional League Northeast last season (3,125) was around twice as high as that in the other leagues. Lok Leipzig themselves had the fourth highest (6,352). More than 11,000 attended the local derby against city rivals Chemie Leipzig.
""Unfortunately, you don't get any points for tradition, crowds and history,"" says 39-year-old Lok chief executive Toni Wachsmuth, who does not have access to sponsors with the same financial firepower as his counterparts further west.
""The [western] leagues do have better economic foundations but everyone agrees that the current system is nonsense. Everyone needs to compromise but some officials are clearly putting their own interests and positions above the general good of German football.""
It comes as little surprise, therefore, that the latest push for change has come from the east, with clubs launching a new campaign in March 2025 with the slogan: ""Meister mussen aufsteigen!"" â champions must go up!
Their proposal: a reduction from five leagues to four, with the precise geographical borders to be determined anew each season with the help of artificial intelligence, taking into account factors such as distance of travel to away games and attractive derbies.
They call it the compass model. Because it can go in all directions. In return, the Regional League Northeast is prepared to compromise by giving up its emotional attachment to the territory of the former East Germany.
""I want to get away from this east-west debate,"" says Tommy Haeder, the 35-year-old chief executive of Chemnitzer FC and figurehead of the reform campaign. ""We've had a change of generation. Many older officials have retired and been replaced by younger people who don't have this nostalgia for the GDR.""
Haeder also emphasises the benefits that a fairer promotion system would bring for German football as a whole, arguing that eastern clubs would be better equipped to continue nurturing youth talent.
""It's not in the interest of talent development when young footballers from Chemnitz are torn away from their homes and families at the age of 10 to join academies in Hamburg, Dortmund or Stuttgart,"" he tells BBC Sport.
""The compass model is a model in which every region sacrifices a little bit to solve a much bigger problem. We're organised, we have a plan, we've made suggestions. Now it's up to the federations to act.""
The campaign has since gained the support of more than 80 clubs across Germany, from east to west and up and down the pyramid.
Union Berlin, who were playing in the fourth tier as recently as the 2000s, became the first Bundesliga club to officially back the initiative, with club president Dirk Zingler saying: ""The current set-up contradicts our understanding of sporting competition.""
Borussia Dortmund president â and Bundesliga chairman â Hans-Joachim Watzke added his influential voice, stating: ""Champions must go up and we think the compass model with four regional leagues is the best option.""
Support also has also come from Bournemouth head coach Marco Rose who, drawing on his past as both a player and coach at Lok Leipzig, said: ""I know what it's like for clubs trying to work within their means, or lack of means, at that level. Everything is on a knife edge. 
""You have to give the clubs and their fans the belief that their efforts and sacrifices will be rewarded with promotion.""
Momentum was growing before a key vote at the end of June when, after months of discussions, clubs were finally set to vote on the compass model â but the ballot descended into farce when, at the last minute, the regional federations conducting the vote announced two key changes:
Suddenly, the four new leagues would consist of only 18 teams rather than 20 (thus making the compass model less attractive because 10 more teams would miss out),  and a stipulation was added that a majority be reached in each of the five regions for the model to pass.
An overall majority (50.9%) in favour of the compass model was still reached (including 60.9% in the West, 61.5% in the North and 76.3% in the Northeast) but the Southwest voted for an alternative option in which the northeastern league would be dissolved and its clubs reallocated, while the Southwest and West would simply remain the same.
""The move from a functioning system to a complex, insecure and economically riskier model without an obvious benefit doesn't make any sense to me,"" Robert Lempka, chief executive of Regional League Southwest side FSV Frankfurt told local public broadcaster Hessischer Rundfunk this summer. ""I find it unsettling that an untransparent algorithm should decide a club's future.""
The Bavarians, meanwhile, voted vaguely for ""further talks"", with Bavarian Football Federation (BFV) President Christoph Kern telling Munich's Abendzeitung newspaper in August: ""We've gathered our clubs together on several occasions and none of the suggested models have majority support. I'm not prepared to simply give up the Regional League Bavaria like that.""
For Haeder in Chemnitz, the vote was ""an absolute catastrophe â completely amateurish"". And he's not alone.
This season, protests have erupted across German football. In the Regional League Northeast, almost every game has begun with a delay as players have simply refused to play for the first minute.
On the opening day, Lok Leipzig supporters, their team still stranded in the same division, threw hundreds of plastic trophies on to the pitch, symbolising the worthlessness of their club's back-to-back titles.
Down in Bavaria, Bayern Munich's ultras attended a reserve team game in the regional league and produced an intricate choreography criticising the Bavarian Football Federation (BFV) for what they perceive to be an unco-operative stance.
""You've taken us for fools long enough,"" read a banner beneath a display depicting BFV officials using a magnet to pull a compass needle towards them. The ultras then set off red pyrotechnics which turned the compass needle back in the direction of the fans.
And before the international break, fans of clubs across the Bundesliga used their platform to call for reform with co-ordinated banners reading: ""Clubs and fans betrayed! Champions must go up! Compass model now!""
The compass, it would appear, may finally be pointing towards a resolution after the German Football Federation (DFB) announced the convening of an extraordinary general meeting on 18 December.
""[The issue] must be resolved,"" said President Bernd Neuendorf. ""The compass model received the most votes and offers the best chance for a concentration of elite performance and therefore better development of talent.""
Any changes would only be implemented in the 2028-29 season â too late for Ziane who, now 34, has embarked on his 13th season with Lok Leipzig. Back-to-back champions, but still in the fourth division.
""You can never completely get over it because, at our level, the 3. Liga is probably the pinnacle. Promotion with my hometown club would have been the most wonderful thing in the world for me,"" he says before heading out for training.
""It's a shame that we have to force ourselves to remember what we've achieved. It's so hard to win this league and we did it twice in a row! You can't take that for granted.""

4,2026-10-02,"For years, CUDA engineers who optimize Nvidia chips have been among tech's most sought-after specialists. Now, they're increasingly managing AI that does it for them.
These engineers specialize in using Compute Unified Device Architecture (CUDA) — Nvidia's software for programming its AI chips, known as GPUs — to write code that can run AI as efficiently as possible.
The jobs are coveted because compute costs are massive; engineers who can squeeze the most value from a chip can save companies millions.
CUDA engineers traditionally spent their days writing kernels — the small pieces of code that tell a GPU how to do a single job as quickly as possible — and then testing them to find the fastest version. Now, AI is taking over much of that painstaking work by generating hundreds of kernels, testing them, and picking the best one.
As a result, CUDA engineers increasingly spend their time supervising coding agents. It's part of a broader shift across the software industry, where developers are moving from writing code to managing AI.
This involves setting goals, checking results, and stepping in when the AI gets stuck, said Jeremy Nixon, founder of the startup Infinity, which builds AI that optimizes chip software.
Because AI can introduce ""bizarre"" bugs that humans wouldn't have written, the job of reviewing AI code has become ""more intense,"" said Anne Ouyang, cofounder of the AI infrastructure startup Standard Kernel.
Despite the shift, hiring data suggests demand for CUDA engineers remains strong. While overall demand for software engineers is lower than in 2023, ""demand for some specialized skills, like CUDA, has grown,"" said Elena Magrini, head of global research at the labor market analytics firm Lightcast.
And scarcity remains a challenge, even as AI has supercharged productivity, said Bing Xu, founder of the AI optimization startup INT21. That's because the deepest CUDA expertise was built over more than 20 years, long before AI sent demand soaring.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
""In the past, we couldn't hire enough good-quality CUDA engineers, and now AI is filling the gap,"" Xu said.
CUDA engineering roles exist both inside and outside Nvidia — including at AI labs and cloud giants. There have already been more US job postings requiring CUDA skills through the first eight months of 2026 than during all of 2025, per Lightcast.
Nvidia remains the largest employer hiring for these positions, according to the firm, with over 300 active US job postings requiring CUDA skills as of September. Public Nvidia job postings for CUDA-related engineering roles advertise base salaries as high as $431,250 before stock and benefits.
Nixon said that AI lowers the barrier to entry for engineers with less experience while simultaneously pushing veterans toward higher-level work, like overseeing AI.
Ouyang said AI could hit junior CUDA engineers hardest, while specialists who can outperform AI and verify its work will become more valuable.
And over time, AI could take on a greater role.
Xu said his company's research had already shown that AI can outperform human engineers on certain benchmarks for writing kernels.
In some cases, AI already writes CUDA code that engineers can't fully understand, though they can verify it's correct, Nixon said.
He said this ability to outperform human engineers offers an early glimpse of ""superhuman"" AI in the real world.

7,2026-10-02,"The company delivered 486,532 vehicles against a consensus near 461,000, while American electric sales collapsed and its European registrations rose 43.3% between January and August on ACEA figures
The Tesla logo on the company’s Fremont factory in California, photographed in October 2010 shortly after it went up
Tesla delivered 486,532 vehicles in the third quarter, down 2% year on year but well above the 461,000 analysts expected, with American electric sales down 47% in August after the federal credit expired. Its European registrations rose 43.3% between January and August, outpacing the wider battery-electric market.
Tesla delivered 486,532 vehicles last quarter, 2% fewer than a year ago and well above what analysts expected. The American electric market has collapsed since the federal credit expired. Europe is where Tesla grew.
Forecasts sat near 461,100, and Tesla’s own compiled consensus at 461,974, as CNBC reported. Shares rose about 2%. Production was 464,391, so Tesla delivered roughly 22,000 more cars than it built.
Last year’s third quarter was the record, inflated by American buyers racing a $7,500 credit that ended on 30 September 2025. Electric sales in the United States were down 47% in August against last year, on Cox Automotive’s figures.
Europe went the other way. Registrations across the EU, Britain and EFTA rose 43.3% between January and August, against 38.8% for the battery-electric market as a whole, according to the European carmakers’ association.
That reverses what we reported in April, when Tesla’s European registrations fell 17% in January, with Norway down 88% and the Netherlands 67%.
September extended the recovery. Portugal rose 128.3%, France 61.9%, Sweden 38.4% and Spain 24.8%.
The market grew underneath it. Electric cars took 29% of new registrations across Europe in August.
Tesla is still smaller here than it was. BYD outsold it across Europe through May, and its share of the EU market has sat around 2%.
Growth and share are different measures. Tesla is climbing fast from a bad 2025 rather than returning to where it was, in a market where Chinese brands hit a record 10.9% in June. Analysts expect that growth to slow to about the market rate next year.
The energy business grew regardless. Tesla deployed 13.7 GWh of storage in the quarter, against 12.5 GWh a year earlier.
Its robotaxi network covers six cities in Texas and Florida. Europe already has a driverless service running on Chinese software, in Zagreb.
The shares are down 21% this year, behind every megacap peer. The Cybercab joined the robotaxi fleet in September and Optimus production is promised before the year ends.
Earnings come on 21 October. Deliveries fell and the shares rose, because the market Tesla was supposed to be losing is the one holding it up.

Founders hear plenty of rules about markets, fundraising and growth. Here are five we chose not to follow.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
Every founder gets advice, and most of it comes with conviction. Find a clear market gap. Raise as much as you can. Get a technical co-founder. Much of it is useful, but very little of it comes with the context that made it true in the first place.
I learned this firsthand while building a company in real estate technology. When we were raising our Series A, an investor told us the market was too competitive and already had too many players. Standard advice for early-stage startups is to find an open lane before going in. That advice made sense in the situations the investor had seen succeed. It didn’t make sense for ours.
Real estate tech was crowded, but it was crowded with mediocre software. There was no clear winner, which meant there was still room to win.
We focused on a segment most people had written off as too small, the top 1% of agents, and built premium software and service for them first. We bootstrapped to $1 million in revenue before raising anything. By the time we took investor money, we understood our customers well enough to know which conventional wisdom applied to us and which didn’t.
That distinction, between advice that’s generally true and advice that’s true for your situation, is something I come back to whenever I talk with founders, whatever their industry.
Most startup advice comes from pattern matching. Someone succeeded doing X, so X becomes gospel. The advice travels faster than the context behind it. The problem isn’t that the advice is wrong; it’s that it stops being advice and becomes instruction.
Wisdom specific to one situation gets turned into a rule everyone follows, whether it fits or not. We chose our own route, knowing we didn’t fit the pattern the advice was designed for. Here are five startup “rules” we broke.
The conventional wisdom is to take the best terms available. There’s logic to that, but raising at 200 times revenue means spending years trying to grow into numbers that were never grounded in your actual business.
We raised at valuations that let us keep the right partners, limit dilution and run a company that worked. Some companies in our market raised too much at valuations that were too high, and they’re now stuck because their last round set a bar that may take a decade to clear. We didn’t want that to be us.
This is a related trap. The argument is that more capital means more runway and more options. In many cases, it actually produces waste and a company that never has to make the hard choices that force you to understand what matters. We raised what we needed to reach the next milestone, plus a buffer, and that discipline paid off.
I’m a solo founder who hired great engineers instead. For a long time, investors flagged that as a structural weakness. I don’t think it is, and with what AI makes possible now, the argument has only gotten weaker. You do need strong technical talent, but you can hire for it.
This piece of startup doctrine has aged poorly faster than almost any other. With AI coding tools, anyone can ship software quickly, so the supply of mediocre products is now essentially unlimited. The only way to win is to build things that are actually great: well-designed, well-tested and genuinely useful.
That’s especially true in high-trust industries. In real estate, customers are making the largest financial decisions of their lives, so the tolerance for broken things was never high. Trust takes years to build and can be lost quickly. Moving carefully where it matters isn’t a concession. It’s a product strategy.
The classic playbook says to enter at the bottom of the market, undercut on price and work your way up. We started at the top and built from there. Those early customers gave us deep product knowledge, a strong reputation and references that eventually carried us further into the market than a low-end entry would have.
None of this was the “right” approach according to startup playbooks. It worked because we understood our customers and market well enough to know where the standard rules applied and where they didn’t.
When you’re early in building a company, you’re surrounded by people with strong opinions about what you should do: investors, advisors and other founders. Much of that advice is offered in good faith. But a lot of it is based on situations that look similar to yours on the surface and are actually quite different. Listening is smart. Accepting it without question, as if context didn’t matter, is the mistake.
A better habit is to treat advice as a prompt for questions rather than a directive. Why does this advice exist? What conditions made it true? Do those conditions apply to my business, my market and my customers? Sometimes they will, and sometimes they won’t. The answer is almost always more valuable than the advice itself.
My recommendation: Find people who have done the specific thing you’re trying to do, in conditions that resemble yours. Ask them why they made their choices, not just what those choices were. The context is the useful part. Without it, you’re following someone else’s map through terrain that may look nothing like theirs.

Every founder gets advice, and most of it comes with conviction. Find a clear market gap. Raise as much as you can. Get a technical co-founder. Much of it is useful, but very little of it comes with the context that made it true in the first place.
I learned this firsthand while building a company in real estate technology. When we were raising our Series A, an investor told us the market was too competitive and already had too many players. Standard advice for early-stage startups is to find an open lane before going in. That advice made sense in the situations the investor had seen succeed. It didn’t make sense for ours.
Real estate tech was crowded, but it was crowded with mediocre software. There was no clear winner, which meant there was still room to win.

Cboe plans to launch KPI-based binary contracts for 23 U.S.-listed companies in October 2026, subject to regulatory approval, with Robinhood set to be the first retail broker to offer them.
The planned products would let investors take positions on company-specific metrics and corporate events. Cboe says the contracts would be SEC-regulated securities traded on its registered U.S. securities exchange, with Robinhood handling retail distribution.
Cboe says its proposed approach differs from similar event-based contracts traded on designated contract markets. It frames the securities structure as offering the transparency, oversight and investor protections of U.S. securities markets.
The companies have not announced a specific list of the 23 companies or detailed which KPIs the initial contracts will cover. Cboe says it will charge no fees on the products through the end of 2026, subject to regulatory review; Robinhood also plans to waive fees through year-end.
The clearing arrangement still requires approval. Cboe has applied to the SEC for temporary registration of its U.S. clearinghouse, Cboe Clear U.S., as a Covered Clearing Agency. If the application is approved, the clearinghouse would clear the KPI contracts.
The planned split gives Cboe responsibility for listing, trading and, subject to approval, clearing, while Robinhood would bring the products to its retail clients. That exchange-and-broker pairing mirrors a broader model in which consumer platforms distribute event products supported by outside market infrastructure, rather than operating the entire market themselves. Robinhood’s use of external market infrastructure provides a relevant comparison.
JJ Kinahan, Cboe’s Head of Retail Expansion and Alternative Investment Products, said the contracts are designed to give investors more granular exposure to corporate metrics and noted that Robinhood would be among Cboe’s first partners bringing the securities to clients.
JJ Kinahan, Cboe’s Head of Retail Expansion and Alternative Investment Products, said the contracts are designed to give investors more granular exposure to corporate metrics and noted that Robinhood would be among Cboe’s first partners bringing the securities to clients.
The announcement also connects the product launch to Cboe’s plans to expand its U.S. clearing capabilities. The company says that, after initial registration and subject to regulatory approval, Cboe Clear U.S. may pursue clearing for products beyond the CFTC-regulated derivatives it clears today, potentially including tokenized binary security options.
Cboe’s proposal places company-KPI event contracts within the securities-market framework, distinguishing them from similar contracts traded on designated contract markets. That distinction is central to the plan: the launch and clearing arrangement remain conditional on regulatory review, rather than being an already-operating product.
The companies announced the plans at Robinhood’s third annual HOOD Summit in Houston on September 30. The proposed October rollout would add company metrics to the event-based products available through Robinhood, while testing whether Cboe’s exchange and clearing infrastructure can support this category of contracts.

11,2026-10-02,"Micron made $37.7bn in net income in three months and has already sold most of next year’s output. The memory shortage is pushing up prices at Raspberry Pi and Samsung.
Micron says the global memory shortage will get tighter over the next two years. The US chipmaker reported record results on Wednesday. Revenue for the quarter to 3 September rose nearly fivefold from a year earlier, to $54.23bn, the company said.
Net income reached $37.7bn, up from $3.2bn a year earlier. Gross margin hit 86.8%, against 44.7%. For the full fiscal year, revenue climbed to $133.19bn from $37.38bn. Analysts polled by LSEG had expected $51.07bn for the quarter, CNBC reported.
“We do not have line of sight to when supply and demand will return to balance,” chief executive Sanjay Mehrotra told investors on the earnings call.
Demand will exceed supply in both 2027 and 2028, Mehrotra said. The market will be tighter in those years than in 2026, he added. About 75% of Micron’s memory output for 2027 is already spoken for. Most current sales talks are about 2028, he said. Much of next year’s high-bandwidth memory (HBM) is sold out. Its prices are “much higher” than this year’s, Mehrotra said.
HBM is the stacked memory inside AI chips. Demand for it is growing faster than for ordinary server memory. Micron is working with Nvidia on what it called the industry’s first custom HBM4E product. It plans new factory clean rooms in 2028. About $25bn of capital spending is set for the first half of its new financial year. Output from new clean rooms ramps up only gradually, Mehrotra said, so they will not quickly ease supply.
DRAM revenue rose 343% year on year to a record $39.8bn, Micron said in its prepared remarks. That was 73% of the total. NAND flash revenue rose 526% to $14.1bn.
The core data centre unit made $18bn in revenue, up from $1.58bn a year earlier. Its gross margin was 90%. The cloud memory unit, which sells HBM, made $16.28bn at an 83% margin. For the current quarter, Micron forecast revenue of $61.5bn, give or take $1.5bn. Analysts had expected $57bn, according to LSEG.
“Micron delivered record fiscal 2026 results, and we expect an even stronger fiscal 2027,” Mehrotra said in a statement.
Mehrotra also said Micron raised pay for every employee in fiscal 2026. Hundreds of its staff in Taiwan had threatened to strike over pay, CNBC noted.
Memory makers are giving priority to AI and server customers, which leaves less for consumer devices. Raspberry Pi has raised the price of its 2GB Pi 4 and Pi 5 by $12.50, citing memory costs. The 2GB Pi 4, once a $35 computer, now costs $67.50. Memory bought cheaply in 2025 helped its record first half, but the company says that stock is largely used up.
Samsung has added $100 to most of its Galaxy S26 range. Samsung executive Kim Taewoo said this week that HBM will take almost 30% of the industry’s DRAM wafer capacity in 2027, up from 20% this year, Reuters reported.

Home > Home Energy & Utilities > Solar
Units that comply with the new law are expected by spring.
Smart Home & Robotics Editor
Smart Home & Robotics Editor
October 2, 2026, 6:24 pm ET
Energy prices are high, no matter what state you live in, but they’re especially high in sunny California. Homeowners in the Golden State have been able to get some relief by installing solar panels to defray their electric bill, and now renters and apartment dwellers can, too, as California officially legalized plug-in balcony solar panels this week. But don’t go buy a kit just yet. The so-called Plug and Play Solar Act (Senate Bill No. 868), signed by Gov. Gavin Newsom and sponsored by state Sen. Scott Wiener, permits the use of plug-in solar for apartments, condos and single-family homes, as long as it meets the regulations in the bill.
“This new law will deliver cleaner power, greater energy independence and real savings for consumers,” Bernadette Del Chiaro, senior vice president for California at the nonprofit Environmental Working Group, said in a statement.
Plug-in solar units don’t have to go on balconies.
Until the passage of bills like this, installing solar panels required you to have an interconnection agreement for anything with the potential to put power back into the grid. In the case of California, the process of completing an interconnection agreement required an upfront cost of $100 to $800 and would take an hour, with approval typically taking three days, according to PG&E.
More from CNET
While the process has been simplified, the new law still has some requirements. The solar system can’t generate more than 1,200 watts per home, which is about enough to power a window AC unit. You also need to register the balcony solar kit, like it’s a rooftop panel system, and it needs to be certified by an outside safety organization. It also needs to be configured not to feed power back into the grid during a power outage, potentially through the use of a separate smart power management meter, such as the Smart Gateway Lite from Mango Power.
Plug-in solar panels can be installed almost anywhere there’s open space and a standard AC outlet. The big advantage of them is that you can deploy them in a backyard, on a balcony or on a roof without having to do the expensive wiring and installation of rooftop solar panels. This technology has been popular in Europe, especially in Germany, where 4 million systems have been installed, but it’s also making steady inroads in the US.
Dozens of US states are considering or have already passed legislation to legalize balcony solar. According to the Environmental Working Group, California is the 10th state to legalize balcony solar, effectively doubling the size of the US market. The first to pass the law was Utah, which did so in March 2025. A more recent one was New Jersey, where Gov. Mikie Sherrill signed a bill that would prevent landlords and local governments from blocking residents and tenants from installing solar panels with a maximum power output of 1,200 watts.
The passage of this law may be a tipping point for balcony solar. When I was at IFA Berlin 2026 in September, I saw several balcony solar systems from Jackery, EcoFlow, Bluetti and others, with a Bluetti representative specifically informing me that they were waiting for some uncertainty around US regulations to be resolved before releasing in the US market.
A representative from Mango Power, which makes solar kits and home backups, told me last year that it was difficult to advise US customers on what solutions are available for their specific states because, ultimately, consumers need to be clear about the regulations before buying.
None of the units currently on the market meet California’s requirements, so you can’t just go out and buy one right now if you’re a California resident. The systems will still need to be registered as rooftop solar systems. Noncompliant systems will also become illegal in the state in 2030, though the groups sponsoring the bill expect that models that are compliant with California’s requirements will come to market in the spring. Various balcony solar manufacturers did not immediately respond to requests for comment regarding the availability of California-compliant units.

		© 2026 CNET, a Ziff Davis company. All rights reserved. 	"
13,2026-10-02,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Carrie Kerpen, cofounder and CEO of Whisper Group. It has been edited for length and clarity.
My parents wanted me to do something I loved that would bring me joy. But they also emphasized that I should find a secure, steady career.
My dad opened a private law practice for a very brief time before leaving to become a law clerk and later a judge. He wanted the security and pension that came with a government job, and he was willing to sacrifice a lot of potential income for that predictability. My mom, a social worker, also had an extremely steady job.
They took a similar approach to money and investing. When the stock market crashed in the 1980s, I heard the story on the news and asked my parents if we were OK. I remember my dad saying I shouldn't worry — my parents didn't invest in that, so we were safe.
My family had a very low-risk, simple life. My parents were content with that. At first I was, too. I was only comfortable taking risks once not taking them seemed intolerable.
I got married for the first time at 24, and it quickly became clear the marriage wasn't going to work. I didn't have the courage to leave until my daughter was born when I was 26. Suddenly, the risk of being a single mom paled in comparison to the alternative: my daughter being raised in an unhappy home.
I took a similar approach at work. I'd been told I would be a great salesperson. Yet I didn't take the leap into sales until the alternative — staying at a lesser-paying job — became too stressful.
My biggest risk was my second wedding, which also turned out to be the launch of my business. When my now-husband David proposed, he told me he wanted a larger-than-life wedding. I didn't like that idea: I was holding shame over my first marriage and divorce.
Still, I wanted David to have the wedding of his dreams, in a way we could afford. So, I took a huge risk: I contacted a minor-league baseball team about renting out their stadium. Then, we sold about $125,000 in sponsorships.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Back in 2006, the idea of a sponsored wedding was totally new and could come across as a little gauche. But our sponsors, friends, and strangers loved it. When sponsors reached out to work with us again, we leveraged it into a media company, Likeable. We scaled the company over time, and sold Likeable in 2020 for eight figures.
I never would have been able to start my company or grow it as I did without accepting risk. I had to unlearn the lessons my parents had taught me about prioritizing security. At first, that was through necessity — like getting my daughter into a happier home.
Later, I learned to calculate the impact of risk. It's a skill I still use today: I reflect on the best-case scenario and the worst-case scenario. As the worst-case scenario is something I can live with, I'm willing to take the risk.
I have these conversations every day with the female entrepreneurs I work with. Too often, female founders don't consider selling their companies because they don't know what they'll do next. Or, they don't acquire other companies because they're unsure how that works. Everything in entrepreneurship is risky, and I wish more women could consider calculated risks without fear.
I also discuss risk with my kids, who are 23, 29, and 11. I've taught them to consider the worst-case scenario and decide whether it's something they're willing to handle. Right now, they have their whole lives ahead of them, and I would like them to take every risk under the sun. There's enough time to self-correct later.

15,2026-10-02,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Your business model needs to be able to adapt when your assumptions are wrong.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
The primary focus of entrepreneurs is thinking about how their business can grow. They model customer acquisition, product expansion, hiring, capital needs, pricing and marketing. What receives less attention is how the business will respond when the assumptions behind those plans change.
Tariffs can alter material or component costs, regulation can reshape an industry, taxes can affect investment economics, and new technologies can disrupt established products. A durable business model should not depend on management accurately being able to predict each one of those changes. It should create enough flexibility to absorb them when they cause disruption. 
The flexibility for external changes reflects differently across industries. A manufacturer may suddenly face higher costs because of tariffs or supply disruptions. A consumer business may encounter rising labor, insurance or occupancy costs that cannot immediately be passed through to customers.
Regulatory changes can increase compliance expenses or make an existing product line less viable. Local or state tax policy or labor expenses can alter headquarters decisions and divestment from a market. Companies operating with little financial or organizational capacity can find that relatively ordinary changes leave them with fewer adjustment options. Large external changes can fundamentally change a business. 
The challenge of external change can be particularly significant for large, established companies. Legacy businesses often possess advantages that newer competitors don’t have, like customer relationships, distribution networks, experienced staff, infrastructure, brand recognition and access to capital. But maintaining those advantages can drain resources if not well-managed.
Capital remains tied to existing products and facilities, systems have been designed around established operating models, and management attention is devoted to overseeing the current enterprise. When technology or customer expectations change in the market, leadership may understand exactly what the company needs to do, but lack the capacity or agility to transition fast enough. 
Resilience should include the ability to invest in change. A manufacturer may need to automate facilities while continuing to support its existing production network. A retailer may need to develop new distribution or digital capabilities before its traditional channels decline. A financial services company may have to replace a core software platform years before the old technology becomes completely unusable. Temporarily, the organization may be financing two versions of the same function during a transition period, but the businesses that focus on competing in a future arena are planning for the long term. 
In the absence of resilience, unforeseen business circumstances, like economic downturns, can cause investment deferments and an inability to leverage capital to adapt to those changes. The inverse is that a strong runway of available capital and staffing resilience can allow companies to quickly pivot in a changing market or address shortcomings that they otherwise would struggle to absorb. Companies that are able to invest during a recession, riding the wave, emerge stronger and better positioned to take advantage of a recovery.
Companies that have optimized every dollar, person or process around current operating models can struggle with transitioning through change. Efficiency is valuable, but there is a point at which eliminating every form of excess capacity can eliminate optionality. If all of the available capital is committed, then adapting to changing conditions becomes substantially harder. The company may eventually be forced to cut costs precisely at a time when it should be investing in innovation. 
Runway planning becomes more important in businesses where substantial investment occurs long before meaningful revenue. Technology or automotive companies may spend years funding engineering, infrastructure, security, testing and product development before reaching scale. Biotechnology companies can face an even longer path involving research, clinical trials, regulatory preparation, manufacturing readiness and commercialization before a successful product generates cash flow.
In those businesses, the central planning focus is understanding how much capital is required if everything occurs on schedule. It also involves what level of contingency is necessary if that schedule fails. 
Management should understand how to adjust if commercialization is delayed, development costs rise, regulatory requirements change or financing markets become less accommodating. A company expecting to need 24 months of capital under its base case should understand what needs to be done if the 36-month path replaces their intended timeline. The objective is to identify financing alternatives, milestones, cost decisions and strategic options before the organization reaches the point where there is no longer a choice. 
There is, however, an important difference between building flexibility and becoming overly cautious. Businesses create value by taking risks. Businesses that preserve capital indefinitely because conditions might change eventually become vulnerable to competitors that continue investing while they stand still. The goal is to take calculated risks without structuring the enterprise so tightly around one expected outcome that a change in assumptions becomes detrimental. 
The most resilient business models preserve optionality. They allow leaders to absorb higher costs, redirect resources and extend investment horizons without immediately needing to sacrifice the future to protect the present. No management team can know exactly how markets, technology, regulation or policy will evolve. The goal is to build a company capable of adapting when its predictions are wrong.
The primary focus of entrepreneurs is thinking about how their business can grow. They model customer acquisition, product expansion, hiring, capital needs, pricing and marketing. What receives less attention is how the business will respond when the assumptions behind those plans change.
Tariffs can alter material or component costs, regulation can reshape an industry, taxes can affect investment economics, and new technologies can disrupt established products. A durable business model should not depend on management accurately being able to predict each one of those changes. It should create enough flexibility to absorb them when they cause disruption. 
The flexibility for external changes reflects differently across industries. A manufacturer may suddenly face higher costs because of tariffs or supply disruptions. A consumer business may encounter rising labor, insurance or occupancy costs that cannot immediately be passed through to customers.

16,2026-10-02,"Donald Trump has said he may ask European countries to release some of their diesel reserves as he considers banning US exports of the fuel.
The president's comment comes after diesel prices hit record highs on UK forecourts this week, as the Iran war continues to put pressure on the supply of refined fuels.
The UK is heavily reliant on imported diesel from the US. But with US voters also facing higher fuel costs ahead of the midterm elections in November, Trump has threatened to restrict diesel exports.
The UK has been discussing the US supply threats with the EU which is holding a meeting on Friday on the issue.
Banning exports would flood the US market with diesel, and could bring prices down significantly for American drivers.
But experts warn such a ban would put further pressure on diesel prices in other countries, unless they can access further supplies.
The US is a vital supplier of diesel to the world, exporting between 1.2 and 1.5 million barrels per day, with most of it going to Latin America.
European countries including France, the Netherlands and the UK, also import a significant amount from the US following the reduction in Russian and Middle Eastern sources of diesel.
Russia's invasion of Ukraine led to sanctions, diverting Russian sales away from Europe. More recently Ukrainian attacks on Russian refining capacity has had a knock-on effect on global supply.
The US-led war on Iran has constrained the flow of both crude oil and refined diesel from that region.
On Thursday, Trump was asked whether he would call on European countries to release some of their own diesel reserves.
""We may do that. They have some diesel,"" he said.
An earlier post from US Treasury Scott Bessent on social media urged Europe to get ready to release some of their own diesel supplies immediately, arguing that US farmers, truckers, and businesses ""should not be left carrying the burden"" as prices soar.
Diesel prices in the UK hit record highs this week and are hovering just under 200p per litre, according to motoring organisation the RAC.
Over half of the UK's diesel is imported, with 31% of the imports coming from the US.
The Netherlands and Belgium together provide more than a third of UK diesel imports.
UK Energy Minister Martin McCluskey was on a call with European counterparts on Thursday to discuss the potential ban.
A source familiar with the discussions told the BBC it was prudent to prepare a co-ordinated response with other countries, including those across EU, but added that there were still European reserves left from a coordinated release of strategic fuel stocks earlier in the year.
""We have a diverse and resilient supply. We continue to engage with our international partners and the UK fuel industry,"" a government spokesperson said. 
The government has stressed that there is no cause for concern about potential diesel shortages, though prices are expected to rise further.
International Energy Agency rules say member states, including the UK, must hold oil stocks equivalent to 90 days of net oil imports.
EU rules say member must hold reserves to cover 61 days of domestic consumption. In total the EU holds nearly 109 million tons of emergency crude and fuel stocks, with around a third in diesel and related fuel products.
A European Commission spokesperson said that there were ""lots of calls, lots of meetings"" taking place on the diesel situation, including with ""high level contacts"" in the US administration.
Potentially adding to the pressure on the global diesel market, Chinese refiners have reportedly halted October exports of some fuel products as Beijing prioritises domestic supplies.Prices of petrol and diesel globally have skyrocketed since the outbreak of the US-Israel war in Iran in February. The closure of the vital Strait of Hormuz, which typically transports around a fifth of the world's oil and gas, has pushed up the price of products made from oil.
An export ban from Russia, also a major supplier of diesel, has added to the pressure on prices.
Trump has argued that keeping any surplus barrels of diesel in the US would lower pump prices domestically, offering immediate relief to drivers, truckers, and businesses ahead of the midterm elections.
But David Fyfe, chief economist at Argus Media, said cutting off American supply would be likely to cause international prices to skyrocket.
Diesel prices have hit an all-time high in the UK, with the latest RAC figures showing average pump prices at 199.79p per litre, up from 142.38p
Diesel is harder to refine than gasoline and, because it is used by the haulage industry and in agriculture, it is very difficult to reduce demand.
The UK has four refineries which produce more than enough petrol to meet demand, they do not make enough diesel to meet the country's needs.
There were 15.1 million diesel vehicles on UK roads at the end of June, according to the Department for Transport. That is a drop from 15.7 million a year before.
There were 9.8 million diesel cars, down year on year from 10.4 million.
Clarification 2 October: An earlier version of this article said that the governing board of the International Energy Agency (IEA) would meet today (Friday).  That sentence was removed after the IEA issued a statement clarifying that no such meeting was planned and it had been announced ""erroneously'"" by a European Commission spokesperson.

The woman alleges she was drugged and raped at a fraternity house at the university two years ago.
The Arizona appeals court ruled that airing an AI message from the dead victim ""crossed that line"".

Google has increased the Pixel 10a's price by $100, pushing its mid-range smartphone to a new $599 starting price.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Google has officially increased the price of the Pixel 10a by $100, meaning the mid-range smartphone now starts at $599 in the U.S.
It's apparently the season of price increases. Just a day after Samsung raised the prices of some Galaxy S26 variants, Google has now increased the price of its mid-range Pixel 10a by $100 (via 9to5Google). The Pixel 10a, which originally launched for $499 in the U.S., is now officially available for $599.
Google hasn't said anything about the reason behind the price increase. But with component costs, particularly memory and RAM, continuing to rise, we presume that's playing a major role here.
When Google launched the Pixel 11 series earlier this year, the company also increased prices by $100 compared to the previous generation. At the time, though, the Pixel A-series remained unaffected. It looks like that's no longer the case, with Google now raising the Pixel 10a's price as well.
This means the base 128GB Pixel 10a now starts at $599 in the U.S., while the 256GB model comes in at $699. The new prices have already kicked in at the Google Store, as well as retailers including Amazon and Best Buy.
 



What makes the price increase particularly difficult to swallow is that the Pixel 10a itself didn't change much compared to last year's Pixel 9a. In fact, it uses the same Tensor G4 processor that first debuted with the Pixel 9 series back in 2024.
Google did make a few improvements, including upgraded wireless charging and satellite connectivity. But paying $100 more for the exact same phone that cost $499 until recently is still a pretty hard pill to swallow.

Unfortunately, that's the smartphone market we're in right now. We expected mid-range and budget phones to be among the devices hit hardest by rising component costs, and with the Pixel 10a's new price, we're now starting to see that play out.

22,2026-10-02,"Google has not said whether European prices follow the American ones, on a phone that is seven months old, runs a chip two generations behind the Pixel 11 and has not changed in any way
Google raised the Pixel 10a from $499 to $599 seven months after launch, with no change to the phone, blaming memory costs that its own vice president said had risen from $2.80 per gigabyte to $12. Apple and Samsung have both repriced existing phones in the past month, and Google has not said whether European prices follow.
Google has raised the price of the Pixel 10a by $100 to $599, seven months after putting it on sale. The phone has not changed. Google has not said whether European prices follow.
The 128GB model went from $499 to $599 and the 256GB version now costs $699, as 9to5Google reported first. The new prices are showing on the Google Store, Amazon and Best Buy. Google did not respond to a request for comment.
The 10a runs a Tensor G4, two generations behind the silicon in the Pixel 11. It arrived in March with limited improvements on the Pixel 9a.
Memory is the reason, and Google has already said so itself.
A company vice president, Shakil Barkat, said in July that RAM had gone from $2.80 per gigabyte to $12, citing Morgan Stanley. The Pixel 11 launched in August at $899, a hundred dollars above the Pixel 10.
Apple moved first on phones already in shops. It raised prices across older iPhones last month, alongside the iPhone 18 Pro.
Samsung then lifted prices by $100 across most of the Galaxy S26 range this week. The practice has spread from new launches to stock on the shelf.
Europe has not been told what happens next.
Currys said in July that its stockpile gave it supply security until at least September, and that phones, laptops and televisions would cost more after that. Alex Baldock, then its chief executive, declined to give a figure. October has arrived.
The retailer sells across the UK, Ireland and the Nordics, and has not announced a rise.
Memory now accounts for as much as half the parts cost of a budget Android phone, against about 15% before the shortage. DDR5 prices in Germany rose 414% in a year.
Google is also cutting memory rather than buying it. The Pixel 11 Pro dropped from 16GB to 12GB at the 256GB tier, and the company is reworking Android to need less.
Nothing cancelled a budget phone in June rather than ship one that was not a step forward. A $599 Pixel on a two-year-old chip is the other way of answering the same problem.

24,2026-10-02,"The chipmaker is worth about $5.7T after a rally of almost 25% from its late July low, and the $300B it needs to reach six trillion is the same sum ASML needs to reach its first, while Europe still makes every machine that prints its chips
Nvidia headquarters in Santa Clara, California
Nvidia hit a record for the first time since May, leaving it under $300B short of becoming the first company worth $6T, on a rally driven by AI agent demand and a record $150B buyback increase. ASML, Europe’s most valuable listed company, needs the same $300B to reach its first trillion.
Nvidia hit a record for the first time since May, and the company is now worth about $5.7T. That leaves it under $300B short of becoming the first company in history worth $6T. Europe’s most valuable company needs the same $300B to reach its first trillion.
Shares rose 2.9% on Friday, as Bloomberg reported. That extends a rally of almost 25% from a late July low. The two-month selloff before it had wiped more than $1T off the company.
The stock is up about 27% this year, a fourth straight year of double-digit returns. Morgan Stanley’s Joseph Moore restored Nvidia as his top semiconductor pick, citing “a very undemanding valuation“.
Two things drove the recovery. Investors expect AI agents such as Meta’s Muse to lift demand for chips, and Nvidia enlarged its buyback.
The company added a record $150bn to the programme on Monday, leaving $235B authorised through fiscal 2028. Apple’s $110B in 2024 was the previous record.
The agent thesis has a second edge. Nvidia also launched a system on Monday meant to stop agents going wrong, and said it would have prevented the breach of Hugging Face by OpenAI’s models.
The Open Agent Safety Platform pairs a sandbox runtime with a watchdog running on Nvidia’s own data processing units. More than 100 companies support it, SAP among them.
Nvidia also owns the company in its own example.
It confirmed on 3 September that it is buying Hugging Face for $12.93bn, and promised to leave it open. So one thesis now sells the chips, the containment and the platform that was breached.
Europe sits on the other side of the trade, holding the equipment rather than the value.
ASML was $300bn from a trillion in July, at roughly $700B, and is Europe’s most valuable listed company. It is the only firm that makes EUV machines, so every advanced Nvidia chip passes through one.
The gap is the same figure in both directions. For Nvidia it is about 5% of its value, and for ASML about 43%.
Nvidia holds $99bn in customer shares, including Intel, SpaceX and OpenAI, up from $7bn a year earlier. Michael Burry says it is overreaching by financing its own buyers. The $6T mark would be a share price, not a shipment.
