Tech > Gaming > Video Games
Developer Fuse Games’ debut release is one of the most impressive arcade racers in recent memory.
Writer
Writer
October 2, 2026, 8:00 am ET
It pains me to say it, but Star Wars’ cultural staying power has taken a massive blow in recent years – and for pretty good reason. Most of the Disney Plus shows have flopped, and the franchise’s return to cinema, The Mandalorian and Grogu, performed worse than any other theatrical release in the series’ history. Despite my lifelong fandom, I cast my eye upon the current state of the sci-fi space opera and feel a sense of apathy.
But Star Wars video games have remained consistently impressive in recent years – the soulslike Jedi: Fallen Order and Survivor duology and the turn-based Star Wars: Zero Company were engaging entries in the canon, prioritizing fun gameplay over anything else.
Developer Fuse Games has continued this string of bangers across wildly different genres with Star Wars: Galactic Racer, an arcade-y game with speedy ships, plenty of underhanded action and a refreshingly self-contained story. It’s F-Zero meets Burnout, and it’s one of my new favorite racing games.
More from CNET
From the outset, I was uncertain if a plotline about a Star Wars racing league would remain narratively engaging over the course of an entire game — its spiritual predecessor, the 1999 podracing classic Star Wars Episode 1: Racer, didn’t bother with one. Once I found out Star Wars: Galactic Racer takes place between Return of the Jedi and The Force Awakens, I was even more skeptical – the sequel timeline hasn’t been an era for consistently engaging stories, after all.
It was a pleasant surprise, then, to discover that Galactic Racer tells a mostly novel story about a small corner of the universe coming to terms with a new normal after the downfall of the Galactic Empire. As it turns out, there’s not much time to engage with speeder racing when entire worlds are buckling under the tyranny of space fascism, so everything has to be put together again from the ground up.
Racing promoter Darius Pax has a passion for high-speed sports, and he’s wrangling sponsors for a new league by pulling in some of the best talent from across the galaxy. That includes shady characters like ex-TIE Fighter pilot Griff Halloran and kindhearted thrillseekers like old Soren Zaks. The most talented racer is nepo baby Kestar Bool, who is using his family’s riches to fund top-tier racing machines and initiate a hostile takeover of Pax’s assets. 
To stave off the imminent threat of Bool seizing the league, Pax enlists the help of main character Shade and their happy-go-lucky mechanic Hibi to win the league championship and keep the sport pure. Shade has a devil-may-care attitude and a mysterious vendetta against the egoistic antagonist, so they’re happy enough to sign on to the competition.
The setup is your classic sports underdog story where you’re looking to score petty vengeance against your sneering opponent. There’s no high-stakes galaxy-level threat – this is a story about outperforming one bad guy on the most dangerous racetracks in the universe, and it rules.
Fuse Games describes the campaign structure as “runs-based,” which essentially means it plays out like many other roguelikes, where random rewards build up your kit as you progress up a procedurally generated tournament bracket. I’m not entirely sold on the integration of this concept for an arcade racer, but I appreciate how crashing out and losing too many events essentially scraps your vehicle and causes Shade and Hibi to begin anew. Shade is supposed to be a scrappy upstart, so losing your parts and upgrades and needing to retune the whole racing kit feels like it serves a narrative purpose.
Galactic Racer does indulge in a little bit of nostalgia: Anakin Skywalker’s podracing rival, Sebulba, appears as a washed-up legend, while the antagonistic Bool family apparently built a large chunk of its wealth running part of the empire’s slave trade. There are plenty of cameos and one-off dialogue lines that will tickle lore nerds pink.
Still, there’s more restraint in this game’s storytelling than in most Star Wars projects, and the narrative is better off for it. I think fans want to explore new corners of the galaxy rather than follow the same handful of families – I know I do – and Galactic Racer debuts plenty of fun characters that don’t connect to high-stakes galaxy-ending plotlines.
The seedy underbelly of the Star Wars universe is perhaps the space opera at its most entertaining, even if that wretched hive of scum and villainy is largely composed of comically evil mobsters and smugglers. It’s great fun to explore that side of the sci-fi world without forcibly including a legacy character like Han Solo or Boba Fett.
There’s a surprising amount of depth to Galactic Racer’s build system. Before you even start a run, you need to decide whether you’re flying a versatile landspeeder, risky speeder bike or graceful skim speeder – each vehicle has different acceleration speeds, turning rates and drifting capabilities.
You’ll start a campaign tournament with mostly stock equipment, but each race won provides credits and upgrades that will help you prepare for the knockout championship event. Different parts provide passive bonuses on the track, and active abilities let you expend the vehicle’s energy at key moments to score advantages in a race.
As with any roguelike, I found a build type that worked for me: I routinely invest in my speeder’s afterburners and its volatile ramjet engine, picking up parts that permit boosting in the dangerous redline zone for longer before worrying about blowing up my craft. There’s still an appreciable level of friction that forces players to make hard decisions – you’re not guaranteed to find parts or abilities you like to use, and crashing or corroding your ship during races can do damage that carries debuffs over to the next event.
More kinetic abilities that encourage brawls with other racers are probably going to be fan favorites, because Galactic Racer has a sickly satisfying takedown system ripped straight out of the old Burnout games. Brushing up against other speeders and watching the sparks fly feels intense, and slamming into an opponent will reward you with a crash cam as they go careening off the track. This type of carnage may not win every run, but building a resilient chassis or dropping bombs to cause mayhem is absolutely cathartic.
That’s not to say that I’m completely in love with every ability you can pick up in the game. The addition of an autopilot is particularly silly to me, especially since drifting around sharp turns and racing clean lines feels gratifying. Worse, while it might buy a couple of seconds to activate traps or fix malfunctioning parts, the ability could just as easily betray you by ending right before you speed directly into a wall. It just isn’t particularly well-implemented – but that’s one of the exceedingly few balancing missteps I discovered.
Lots of thought was put into the roguelike structure of Galactic Racer, and it feels like there’s a viable path for every type of racer to win – whether you enjoy running clean lines or using underhanded tactics to force opponents off the track.
There’s plenty of content for budding racers to take on once they’ve taken Kestar Bool down a few pegs in Galactic Racer’s story mode. The game features arcade time trials, multiplayer races, podracing events and more cosmetic rewards than you can shake a rathtar tentacle at. These are genuinely challenging races that could take hours to score a gold medal on, and I started to feel like a bit of a masochist after half a dozen attempts to master the ramjet-themed arcade event.
A notable pain point is the lack of an AI quick race – it seems like a massive oversight that there’s no mode where you can hop on, pick a track and difficulty and duke it out in a single match against some bots. This is something that I hope gets rectified soon.
A few flaws notwithstanding, it’s obvious that the development team at Fuse Games has an earnest love for this storied universe, and it shows through plenty of little tidbits and extra flourishes that flesh out the gameplay experience. Runs build into their own diegetic narratives as players crash out opponents and form rivalries that last throughout multiple races.
Characters react to your skill (or lack thereof), challenging Shade to one-on-one knockout races and proposing lucrative side bets before each event. It makes sense that any headstrong individual willing to strap themselves into a speeder bike and speed through the wreckage of a downed Star Destroyer would arrogantly put their money where their mouth is, and it makes the world feel alive.
Many of those elements carry over to the multiplayer tours, where players are able to gamble among themselves with credits earned in previous races. I can foresee this potentially getting a tad toxic in a public lobby, but in my experience, it was a great way for emergent narratives to form and made the mode feel all the more meaningful.
I can’t imagine this game will make many critics’ GOTY lists, especially when so many blockbuster projects have already been released. But Star Wars: Galactic Racer is easily one of my favorite games of 2026 – and not just because its gameplay loop had me inadvertently staying up for “one more run” at 3 a.m.
Among its many virtues, the game reminded me that there’s still value in a galaxy far, far away. Over breakneck races and loving details, my apathy for the series melted away, returning a childlike spark of awe and wonder for this sci-fi universe. I doubt a game like this will dictate the future creative direction of a juggernaut franchise like Star Wars, but Galactic Racer is surely something special for anyone who wants to ride their ramjet from Crait to Tatooine.
Star Wars: Galactic Racer is set to be released for PlayStation 5, Windows and Xbox Series X and S on Tuesday.

52,2026-10-02,"You never know what kind of impact it might have on your former company when you hand in your notice. Many managers dread seeing the back of a decent employee whom they rely on, but that doesn't necessarily mean that they treat them in a way that makes them want to stay, either. At its most chaotic, the departure of an employee can be something that causes a whole team or even an organization to collapse.
At the same time, nobody should be guilted into staying in a role that is no longer working for them. It is the nature of the modern job market for workers to change employers and try to get the best deal for them by shopping around. If a boss can't handle the reality of that, they are not prepared for the way the world works in this day and age. People move on.
That being said, not every manager is going to let an employee go so easily. The nicest way to do that is with a generous counteroffer, but unfortunately, some don't believe in the kinds of tactics that a worker might actually appreciate.

53,2026-10-02,"Photo with Quantum X Labs scientist Nir Sharon

GPS is reliable until someone interferes with the signal. Quantum X Labs scientist Nir Sharon (also associate professor at Tel Aviv University) argues quantum gyroscopes and miniaturized atomic clocks can reduce the cumulative error that has always limited dead reckoning, giving autonomous vehicles, aircraft, and defense platforms intrinsic navigation that retains useful positioning when satellites are unavailable. The company has scientific prototypes in the lab and is working to translate them toward product applications.
GPS has become so embedded in modern life that its infrastructure is almost invisible. A driver opens a map, an aircraft follows its navigation system, or an autonomous machine calculates its position, and the underlying infrastructure disappears from view. Nir Sharon, Chief Quantum Technology Scientist at Quantum X Labs and an associate professor of applied mathematics at Tel Aviv University, argues that this apparent reliability conceals a structural weakness.
“The problem with GPS is almost the success of GPS itself,” Sharon explains. “People see a system that works silently and accurately, so they assume the infrastructure underneath is equally robust. Yet the signal can be blocked or spoofed with surprisingly little effort. A system can appear perfectly stable until someone interferes with the signal it depends on.”
In his view, the vulnerability is harder to ignore as navigation systems move into environments where positioning errors carry consequences. Sharon notes that aircraft, autonomous vehicles, agricultural machinery, and defense platforms require continuous and dependable location data. A navigation system that loses its external reference, he argues, can leave a machine with no reliable way to establish where it is.
That problem becomes more consequential once time enters the equation. Precision clocks underpin synchronization across sophisticated systems, including communications and navigation infrastructure. Sharon argues that the question is not simply whether a device can tell the time, but how accurately it can measure it when synchronization itself becomes part of the system’s performance.
“People often ask why we need such an accurate clock when we already have one on our phone or computer,” he says. “The need becomes clear in systems that require precise synchronization. Navigation works the same way. If you know where you are and can measure how you move, you can keep navigating even when the external signal disappears.”
The underlying concept is remarkably old. Dead reckoning, Sharon explains, allows a system to estimate its position from a known starting point by measuring direction and movement. He argues that its weakness is cumulative error. Small inaccuracies may compound with every movement, gradually expanding uncertainty around the estimated position.
Quantum sensing, Sharon notes, offers a route toward reducing that error. Quantum gyroscopes can use quantum phenomena to make extremely sensitive measurements of rotation and motion, while quantum-enabled clocks can provide highly precise timing references. The objective is a new generation of sensors capable of supporting navigation that can retain useful positional information when satellite signals become unavailable.
Quantum X Labs, an Israeli multidisciplinary quantum technology company, is developing technologies across quantum computing, quantum software and quantum sensing. Its sensing portfolio includes a quantum gyroscope and a miniaturized atomic-beam rubidium clock. According to Sharon, the company has scientific prototypes and proof-of-concept systems operating in laboratory environments, with work underway to translate them toward product applications.
Sharon characterizes the present challenge as increasingly an engineering problem. The scientific foundations are advancing, while commercialization requires sensors to become sufficiently compact, robust, and application-specific for industries that have different performance requirements.
“Developing the science is one part of the journey,” Sharon says. “Turning that science into something an automaker or an aircraft manufacturer can actually integrate requires a different level of engineering and market understanding. We are working with partners because the path from a laboratory proof of concept to a product requires understanding exactly what the end user needs.”
The implications reach into autonomous systems in particular. Sharon explains that a vehicle operating at speed cannot simply stop because its satellite positioning has disappeared, or a drone or robot entering an enclosed environment may have no continuous external positioning signal at all.
Sharon sees intrinsic navigation as a critical component of the next generation of autonomous systems. “At some point, you have to ask what happens when GPS is unavailable,” he says. “If an autonomous car reaches a situation where its system says, ‘We have no GPS location, so we cannot continue,’ that is a fundamental limitation. An intrinsic navigation system could allow the vehicle to keep operating, at least well enough to reach a safe destination.”
Quantum sensing, in his view, points toward a shift in how navigation infrastructure is conceived. GPS may remain an important reference, but systems that can independently measure motion, orientation, and time could provide the resilience required by increasingly autonomous machines.
Quantum X Labs is developing toward that future with a portfolio spanning sensing and other quantum technologies, supported by a network that includes academic and industry collaborations. The larger question is whether the next generation of machines can afford to depend on a single external signal for something as fundamental as knowing where they are.

55,2026-10-02,"His visit came amid low national approval ratings.
Fresh off his visit to Texas and Oklahoma, President Donald Trump rallied supporters in Mobile, Alabama, on Friday night. It was the latest stop on a swing through deep-red states to boost midterm election turnout among Republican voters and thank them for their support, amid low approval ratings for the president.
Trump said he he wanted to ""pay respect"" to his supporters and announced plans to return to the state next week for the Georgia-Alabama college football game. 
In his remarks, Trump touted the economy, defended the Iran war and again told voters to pretend that he is on the ballot.
""When I'm not on the ballot, we don't do well. ... When I'm on the ballot, we kill everybody,"" he said. ""So just close your eyes and pretend I'm on the ballot.""
Trump acknowledged higher oil prices amid the war with Iran, but downplayed it as a ""small price"" to pay to prevent Iran from having a nuclear weapon. He also said prices would likely drop after midterm elections, which is when he predicted the Iran war will ""probably"" end. 
""But remember about the oil: You're paying a price, but boy, is that a small price than what it would be if these lunatics were allowed to have a nuclear weapon and used it on Mobile, Alabama,"" Trump said.
Trump won Alabama three times: by 27.7 points in 2016, 25.5 points in 2020 and 30.5 points in 2024.
In his remarks Friday, the president also nodded to his history with Mobile, a city that holds political significance for him. In August 2015, at his first rally in the city, then-Sen. Jeff Sessions of Alabama publicly endorsed him-- support that became a key moment on Trump's path to the Republican nomination.
""I don't know if you know. It sort of all began here,"" Trump said.
On Thursday, Trump explained why he is spending time in states he has carried easily.
""I'm going because you have to thank these states that are with you 1,000%,"" Trump said. ""And I haven't been there in a while, and I want to just thank them.""
According to an AP-NORC poll, Trump's job approval rating stands at a record-low over both of his terms as president at 31%, with 69% of Americans disapproving.
Mobile was redrawn after mid-decade redistricting to be in Alabama's 1st Congressional District. The redrawn district, favoriting Republicans, would have voted for Trump in 2024 by around 40 points.
Trump came back to Mobile after winning the 2016 election and held an event at Ladd-Peebles Stadium as part of his post-election ""thank you"" tour.
Rep. Barry Moore, now the Republican nominee for Senate, first backed Trump at the 2015 rally when Trump was running in a crowded presidential primary field.
""President Trump knows how important it is to have rock-solid conservatives representing Alabama in Washington and Montgomery, and I'm happy to have him visit our state so close to this critical midterm election,"" Moore said on social media.
A Republican National Committee official made the case that Mobile was chosen partly for its reach beyond Alabama. The Mobile-Pensacola media market covers the western Florida Panhandle. The RNC said it expects the rally to energize voters and generate coverage in both states.
Several other lawmakers joined Trump at the rally, including GOP Sens. Tommy Tuberville, running for governor, and Katie Britt. Former Rep. Jerry Carl, the GOP nominee in the solidly Republican 1st District that now includes Mobile, called the visit a ""great honor.""
The House seat to watch
The race to watch in Alabama is the state's 2nd Congressional District. There, Republican state Rep. Rhett Marques, who is set to speak Friday, is challenging Democratic Rep. Shomari Figures.
The district's lines changed after the Supreme Court's ruling in Louisiana v. Callais weakened the Voting Rights Act. Following that ruling, Alabama reinstated its 2023 congressional map, which federal courts had previously found discriminatory against Black Alabamians.
Figures won the seat in 2024 with 54.6% of the vote under a court-drawn map. Now the Cook Political Report calls him the ""clear underdog,"" and ABC News rates the race as likely Republican. It is one of several contests that could help decide control of the House.
After Alabama, the president will rally Saturday in Ohio, which is the center of a competitive Senate race between incumbent GOP Sen. Jon Husted and former Sen. Sherrod Brown, a Democrat.
After stumping for Texas GOP Senate candidate Ken Paxton on Thursday, Trump will return to the Lone Star state on for a rally in San Antonio.
Trump's return to Texas signals Republicans' worries about the Senate race, where Paxton is running in a toss-up race against Democrat James Talarico.

56,2026-10-02,"The Nvidia Shield TV is an iconic piece of Android hardware but it also hasn’t seen a refresh in the better part of a decade. But, because of the increasingly depressing state of the entire tech industry, Nvidia Shield TV Pro is getting a $100 price hike to $299, seven years after it first launched.
Nvidia Shield TV Pro made its debut in October 2019 and, to this day, remains one of the most capable Android TV streamers on the market. While certain aspects haven’t aged perfectly, such as a lack of AV1 support and others, it remains unmatched in other places.
That’s a big part of the reason that, even so many years after it launched, Nvidia continues to produce the Shield TV.
But the $199 price point is gone. Effective today, Nvidia Shield TV Pro now costs $299.99. Early this year, Nvidia said that it would continue software support for the Shield TV for the forseeable future. 
Shield isn’t the only streamer going up in price as a result of RAMageddon. Google TV Streamer saw a price hike to $149 recently, while other devices including Apple TV 4K and various Roku streamers also saw price hikes earlier this year.

You're reading a newsletter from The Indicator from Planet Money. Once a week, we curate our favorite stories and insights on business, finance, economics and why they matter. Subscribe here to get it in your inbox every Friday.

Matt Robb's conversation with his Meta AI agent Jarvis

Screenshot via @matt.j.Robb, permission given/Threads

This week, a guy had random buyers show up to his house thanks to Meta's new AI agent Muse! A cautionary tale for you... Matt Robb, a YouTube creator, posted online that he let Muse manage his Facebook Marketplace account. When he let Jarvis (the name of this AI agent) sell a wireless keyboard, things did NOT go as planned. The bot accepted a low-ball offer and consequently handed out Matt's ADDRESS to a potential buyer. 
The Indicator team has been talking about Muse a lot this week after the app popped off with around 5 million downloads since its Sept. 8 launch. Is it trustworthy? How useful can it really be? Host Wailin Wong says her mom is already finding a use for it! 
""My mother named her Muse agent after Mr. Darcy from Jane Austen's Pride and Prejudice. She had Darcy design a greeting card for a relative and a t-shirt as an 80th birthday gift for someone in her senior living community,"" Wailin says. ""Then she got them printed at the public library maker-space. I never imagined my mom would use an AI agent!""
Coming up:
Trivia Question: What is one mechanism hedge funds use to help people sidestep paying the government? Email us indicator@npr.org.
What to know: Bloomberg wrote this week that American consumers are spending less on bowling, arcades or amusement parks. With inflation tightening people's purse strings, spots like Dave & Buster's and Lucky Strike are seeing less traffic and it's hurting their bottom line. Six Flags is seeing lower attendance, too.
On the OTHER hand, CNBC wrote how consumers are spending more on hobbies at home compared to last year. People are making more transactions AND hobbies are costing more. We're talking arts & crafts, video games, books, the list goes on.
So IS fun over?: Of course not, but people may be substituting treks out of the house for more in-home activities. It's pricey as heck to travel right now, whether you're driving or flying!
So is it getting more expensive to have fun? Whether it's out of the house, or in the house, it's costing more. But people are willing to spend on more localized activities.
One interesting note from Bank of America's analysis on the price of fun: people with the least free time are spending the most on hobbies, aka older millennials. Part of the reason: they're buying for their kids too.
What to know: When you go to the gas station, diesel is often the pricier option compared to unleaded or premium gas. Can you remember a time when it was less expensive?? Right now, the price of diesel is a BIT of an aberration. The national average this week is $6.38... last year, the average was $3.60. It's gotten so bad, Republican members of Congress have been lobbying President Trump to ban diesel exports in hopes of lowering prices. Apparently, the administration is seriously weighing the move, though the American Petroleum Institute is adamantly against it.
REGARDLESS! Diesel prices are consistently higher than unleaded or premium gas prices. Why is that?

                Diesel fuel has been pricier than regular gasoline, with a few exceptions, since 2004
                
                    
                    David Zalubowski/AP Photo/David Zalubowski/AP
                    
                
hide caption

When diesel got expensive: This week, diesel fuel costs just under $2 more than regular gasoline per gallon. But once upon a time, diesel actually cost LESS than regular-grade gas. The switch happened back in Sept. 2004. The Energy Information Administration points to three primary reasons.
Demand: Back in the early 2000s, China and India were motorizing and industrializing at new levels, according to Clay Seigl, who leads the energy practice at Beacon Global Strategies. He adds they were ramping up agriculture alongside big construction and infrastructure projects too. That's all powered by diesel.
At the same time, in Europe, diesel grew from just 13.8% of new car registrations in 1990 to 53% in 2007.
It's worth noting though, that nowadays, the price hike on diesel is less about demand, and more about supply. There's a lot less diesel due to the Iran and Ukraine wars limiting refining capacity and crude production, and yet the need for diesel fuel is still essential from trucks and trains to generator farms and heating.
Air quality standards: In June 2006, federal regulation kicked in that reduced the maximum amount of sulfur that could be included in diesel fuel. The hope? Reduce air pollution. But it costs more to produce ultra-low sulfur diesel due to higher refining and distribution costs. This came after 2004, but may have contributed to higher prices later on.
Taxes: In 1993, the federal excise tax increased to 24.4 cents per gallon for on-highway diesel. That's 6 cents more than the equivalent for gasoline.
If you HAVEN'T listened: Back in 1984, Doug Flutie threw a Hail Mary pass in the final seconds of a college Bowl game against Miami. He made the pass and Boston College was victorious. The win boosted the entire college's admissions, starting something called the ""Flutie effect."" The idea: when your sports programs do well, your whole admissions does well.
Nowadays, the stakes are high for colleges to increase enrollment. In the coming years, fewer high school students are expected to go to college in the U.S. So … universities are investing in their sports programs and advertising them hard. Is it working?
LISTEN TO THE EPISODE
If you HAVE listened: Doug Chung, our Flutie Effect researcher, found if a school elevates their sports team from ""mediocre"" to ""great,"" it boosts their applications by nearly 18%. BUT … there are other proven ways to get to that same figure.
Schools can:
Our reporter Ricky Mulvey's take-away: ""Football is the flashiest part of a school, but boring economic decisions get more people coming through.""

Listener Laura sent in a question from Belton, TX
Question: I just listened to your episode on AQR tax-loss harvesting, which appears to only be available to the rich. Could you discuss in an episode the difference between that and direct indexing which also involves tax loss harvesting? Direct indexing has been available on sites like Public.com for as little as $1,000 recently.
Answer: Oh, the ways we Americans can avoid paying the government. So numerous! Laura, you are right, direct indexing is another way to do tax-loss harvesting, distinct from the tax-aware long-short strategy we talk about in the episode. So let's break it down.
I spoke with Alex Matturri, who used to run the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Indices, to help me understand. He explains that direct indexing, at a basic level, is just customizing an index. For example, you might start with S&P 500 and then only choose companies for ESG purposes. But when it comes to saving money on your tax bill, you can build a portfolio GEARED towards tax efficiency. And you don't need a ton of money to do that.
That's different from what AQR is doing, which is much more sophisticated. But at the basic level, the tax-aware long-short strategy is both buying stocks and shorting some of them to create losses that can then offset your tax bill. Thank you for the question, Laura!

Producer Julia Ritchey is watching silent films at her local arthouse theater 

The Wurlitzer itself on stage at the Lucas Theatre.

Julia Ritchey

hide caption

Opened in 1921, the Lucas Theater in Savannah, Ga., regularly screens old movies that have been digitally restored. Notably, the theater also houses its original Wurlitzer pipe organ from 1925 that would regularly accompany movies during the silent film era. Of the 2,100 or so Wurlitzer pipe organs produced by the Wurlitzer Company up until 1943, fewer than 15 remain in the building where they were originally installed, let alone restored and played regularly! Unlike other pipe organs, these engineering marvels have percussive sound effects for thunderclaps, horse hooves and train whistles, perfect for old movie palaces.
For the 100th anniversary screening of Buster Keaton's The General, considered one of the greatest comedic films ever made, the Lucas' organist scored the entire 75-minute film without one break, including several of Keaton's legendary locomotive stunts. It was a delightful cinematic treat for any film buff.

58,2026-10-02,"The World Architecture Festival celebrates one-of-a-kind buildings from around the world each year.
A panel of international architects and experts reviews entries on their way to crowning a ""Building of the Year."" Each year, WAF releases a shortlist of entries that showcase some of the world's most notable buildings.
Many projects were submitted, but only 416 made WAF's 2026 shortlist. There are four major categories: completed buildings, future projects, interiors, and landscape.
Once the shortlist is decided, finalists present their projects to judges — this year, they'll do so in Fort Lauderdale, Florida, in November.
The completed building entrants are competing for the title of ""Building of the Year."" The festival's 164 judges, representing 37 countries, will judge completed buildings grouped into categories such as civic and community, housing, and office.
Business Insider reviewed the completed-building shortlist and selected 18 of the most striking architectural projects, from brand-new structures to reimagined historic places, to showcase some of the world's most extraordinary recent designs.
Much has been said about JPMorgan's new headquarters, which opened on Park Avenue in New York City in October 2025. It's a 60-floor behemoth designed for 10,000 employees.
The building, designed by Foster + Partners, is an all-electric tower and has 2.5 million square feet of ""flexible and collaborative space,"" the World Architecture Festival wrote.
Alila Mayakoba was chosen as a finalist in the retrofit category — it was an existing luxury resort that was extensively renovated between 2025 and 2026.
The resort is located in Riviera Maya, Mexico, and the redesign was spearheaded by Cuaik CDS.
The crown jewel, the redesigned arrival pavilion, centers on a reflecting pool that brings natural light and ventilation into the lobby, according to WAF. Across the property, the restaurants, wellness facilities, and social gathering places were all redone with community in mind.
The Amami House was originally built as a residence on Amami Island, Japan, about 236 miles from the mainland. The structure is completely off the national power grid and relies on solar energy.
The architects designed the home so its owners could live independently in response to concerns about climate change, population decline, and environmental pressures, according to WAF. Sakai Architects considered other energy solutions, such as wind, hydro, and geothermal, but solar was its best option.
Other energy-conscious design choices include a fully raised structure and a layered roof to improve airflow and reduce heat.
Aqualuna is an apartment building in Toronto, designed by the architectural firm 3XN GXN. It's located on Toronto's waterfront with views of Lake Ontario (or Lake America, depending on who you ask) and features 215 residential units, each with outdoor spaces such as terraces or balconies.
The building's design was inspired by Danish apartments with a central courtyard that houses shared amenities, fostering community, per WAF.
Ayana, which is camouflaged by its surroundings and hard to see from above, almost looks like it's home to an underground society, which is kind of the point for this boutique resort in Sholayur, India.
Aslam.sham architects built the 19-room resort into the grasslands in a way that follows the contours of the landscape and with features that frame the outdoors for guests, WAF wrote.
Citadella was a fortress at the top of a hill in Budapest to keep the city in line in case a revolution broke out — the last one was in 1848. In the 1960s, some renovations were made to convert a portion of the building into a hotel, but by the beginning of the 21st century, it had been abandoned.
The Hungarian-based architectural firm art1st design studio Kft took over in 2021, refurbished the building, and turned it into The Bastion of Liberty, a museum and free park for the city.
Located in Anjarle, Maharashtra, India, overlooking the ocean, Crest Nine is a community center for a gated community.
Designed by Sanjay Puri Architects, the curved wellness structure is home to a sports room, a gymnasium, a restaurant, a bar, and four guest bedrooms, according to WAF. It also has three pools, a health club, and an open cafeteria.
The Folia was chosen as a finalist for mixed-use buildings — WAF wrote that it contains ""a hotel, offices, retail, and apartments."" It was built in Zhejiang Province, China (south of Shanghai) and designed by ET Architects.
The five buildings are designed as steps of various heights to reflect the area's surrounding bamboo forest.
In a joint venture between TEAM Architects and 242 AM Architects, the two firms created a home for an exotic plant collection that looks more like a sanctuary.
The Lath House sits in Palmerston North, New Zealand, and is essentially a display of plants from around the world, but it also serves as a conservatory with a circular shape that naturally leads visitors through the space.
The Mehrangarh Fort Visitor Centre isn't like other visitor centers you'd see on the side of a highway.
The essence of a fort remains largely intact, but the architect, Studio Lotus, created spaces that make visitors feel more welcome, with walkways, courtyards, and rejuvenated historical features.
Mercado Nicolás Bravo, in Quintana Roo, Mexico, is a crafts market designed by AIDIA Studio.
In addition to being a marketplace, it's also a community center dedicated to showing visitors and tourists the area beyond the region's better-known Maya archaeological sites.
The roof is modeled like inverted umbrellas, which are designed to collect rainwater for irrigation.
Many of the buildings on WAF's list have water features, but few make water the main feature, like the Modular Hospital Ship. As the name suggests, the Worldwide Hospitals Modular Hospital Ship is a deployable medical city that can be used to aid areas during humanitarian crises or after a disaster.
The interior of the vessel is occupied by modular units housing various rooms, such as an operating space, a full radiology suite, and ICU stations, among others. The ship was built by Pocket Hospital Switzerland AG.
Durham, North Carolina's Murray-Massenburg Elementary School can't be missed after EVOKE Studio Architecture's work on it.
The school is arranged around a central courtyard instead of long traditional corridors, which can be overwhelming for students, WAF wrote.
It also features a rooftop garden that reduces runoff and functions as an outdoor classroom.
Prestige University looks like a collection of never-ending stairs rising nearly 100 feet. The building is supposed to mirror the look of India's stepwells — steps that lead down to underground wells or other water sources.
The five-level building, designed by Sanjay Puri Architects, features administrative offices, an open-air auditorium, seminar halls, a library, and a cafeteria, per WAF.
Tucked away in rural Vadodara, India, the Self Sustaining Farmhouse's claim to fame is in the name. The home, designed by Manoj Patel Design Studio, is mostly made from brick, natural stone, reclaimed wood, and recycled materials, per WAF.
The building features several open passages that connect different parts of the structure without disrupting the surrounding environment.
Foster + Partners worked on Cambodia's Techo International Airport.
To fulfill its goal of being ""one of the greenest airports in the world,"" the terminals are filled with trees, plants, and natural light, according to WAF. It will also be run on energy from a nearby solar farm.
The Xinchang Globular Center, located in the Chinese city of Shaoxing, was designed to be equally useful when it's not being used for professional sports.
To address that, architects line+ studio conceived the ""Sports Mall"" — this building has all the typical sports arena facilities, but also restaurants, shops, and recreation spaces, per WAF.
The large tower contains tracks, more indoor fields, and viewing platforms.
The Yazhou Bay Hydroacoustic Measurement and Testing Center was designed by the Architectural Design & Research Institute of Zhejiang University. It's located in Sanya, a city on the island of Hainan, off the coast of China.
The building itself is an underwater research facility, and it was built around a giant water tank and the adjacent lab space, according to WAF.

analysis
The Business
Topic:Stock Market
Tim Rosenfield and Oliver Curtis are founders of Firmus Technologies. (ABC News: Kelsey Reid)
As redemption tales go, they don't come any bigger than that of Oliver Curtis.
A decade ago, Curtis was a corporate pariah. Convicted of conspiracy to commit insider trading, he was sentenced to two years in prison.
Sent to a relatively low-key facility in Cooma, where it is said he became a hairdresser for inmates, he was released after one year on good behaviour.
Stockbroker Oliver Curtis and his wife Roxy Jacenko arrive at the NSW Supreme Court, June 17, 2016. (AAP: Joel Carrett)
By next Friday, Oliver Curtis is expected to emerge a billionaire; the driving force behind one of Australia's biggest ever stock market listings, second only to Telstra's privatisation in the 1990s.
If all goes to plan, Firmus, the data centre builder and operator which has existed in various forms for just seven years, will raise $7.2 billion in new capital as part of the listing.
When added to the existing capital, the Singapore based Firmus will be valued at an incredible $43.7 billion.
Already, it boasts some of the world's most powerful backers. US investment giant Blackstone has close to 7 per cent of the existing stock while chip giant Nvidia — which routinely vies for pole position as the world's most valuable corporation — holds slightly more.
AI data centre developer Firmus Technologies is seeking investors ahead of a multi-billion-dollar share market float next month.
Curtis, however, will be the big winner.
With more than 13 per cent of the company, he will officially cement his role on the rich list.
His father Nick, a pioneer of Australian critical minerals mining and the driving force behind Lynas Rare Earths, is also slated to profit handsomely from his 5.6 per cent stake.
But the float, and the industry, has its detractors.
Amid the ongoing investor frenzy for all things AI, there is a growing apprehension on artificial intelligence from governments globally while on the data centre front line, community opposition could dent the rosy forecasts for future earnings.
And then there are the financial risks, and the circular financing and interdependence within the industry; risks highlighted this week by the Reserve Bank of Australia.
Oliver Curtis and business partner Tim Rosenfield have faced more obstacles than most in their quest to convince the globe's biggest tech players and investors to jump aboard the Firmus express.
There is the uncomfortable reality that neither has any experience running data centres or a background in technology.
But foremost among them, is Curtis's past.
His transgression wasn't a one-off or a simple spur of the moment lack of judgement.
Sydney stockbroker Oliver Curtis is jailed for two years after being found guilty of conspiring to commit insider trading.
He and his former best friend from school, John Hartman, engaged in a cleverly planned and executed scheme to profit from insider trades.
In 2007, just before the global financial system began its meltdown, Hartman approached Curtis with a plan.
Whenever his employer, investment group Orion, was about to engage in a large transaction, he would notify Curtis to trade in what is known as Contracts For Difference, which ordinarily are high risk instruments that routinely clean out investors.
Not so for Curtis.
According to the evidence, over the course of a year, he engaged in these trades on 45 separate occasions, trousering a total profit of more than $1.43 million. 
The then 21-year-olds split the profits with Curtis splurging on lavish gifts for his mate.
But in 2009, not long after the corporate regulator began investigating Hartman, he spilled the beans and was the first to do time.
He has admitted in interviews that his time behind bars was tough and that it deeply impacted his family.
In the AI lexicon, Firmus is what's known as a neocloud.
Traditional data centres, the kind employed until now by the likes of Google, Meta, Amazon et al, are designed to handle a range of applications, everything from search engines to database management and content storage.
Neoclouds are data centres equipped with Graphics Processor Units (GPUs) and specifically designed to cater to the needs of artificial intelligence.
And the key point of difference, the selling point, between Firmus and its rivals is an innovation that allows it to use a mere fraction of the water than that required by the existing technology.
It also claims to have the lowest operating costs in the industry. Ask a question about what AI centres actually produce, and you'll be bombarded with constant references to ""tokens"".
Tasmania Premier Jeremy Rockliff alongside Firmus co-CEOs Tim Rosenfield and Oliver Curtis at the Firmus Technologies facility in Launceston. (Supplied: Tasmanian government)
It's simply a measure of a neocloud operator's output. And according to Firmus, their AI tokens are about the cheapest on the planet.
It's that selling point that has attracted the likes of Meta and OpenAI as customers, Blackstone as an investor and Nvidia as a supplier and investor.
The company, however, is still making a loss. And the planned rollout of its technology will require a huge amount of capital expenditure. Which is why it wants to list on the stock market.
It hopes to turn a profit within two years, according to its prospectus, with about $US5 billion ($7.2 billion) in annual earnings from its data centres in Malaysia, Indonesia along with the centres it is developing in Tasmania.
Stock markets live and breathe hype.
There's been plenty of that with Firmus which, along with its well-heeled backers, have helped generate a whirlwind of FOMO among would be punters.
It's also created concerns among regulators.
The RBA, back in July, said it was concerned that the huge spending spree on data centres may create shortages of workers, materials and capital, thereby adding to the inflation wave that has beset the economy.
This week, it added another concern.
In its Financial Stability Review, the RBA highlighted the threat to the global economy from so called neocloud companies, and particularly in the way they secure finance.
In the past 18 months, there have been numerous round robin deals between the big tech firms, chip makers and data centre operators. They invest in each other, and they buy products from each other.
It's almost a closed shop.
A data centre that received recent council approval to have diesel generators as backup power is now appealing against the ""unreasonable"" conditions on how they can be used.
And given they are the world's most valuable companies, the RBA is concerned that a problem in one could ricochet through the entire industry, thereby triggering global stock market instability which could then impact the financial system.
""Some cloud providers have invested in AI developers who subsequently use those providers' infrastructure to train and deploy their models,"" it said.
Like its US competitors, Firmus has happily exploited this model of extreme interdependence.
The big question is whether Firmus's impending stock market debut will deliver for those who tip their cash in once it becomes a public company or simply is a means for early investors to cash out.
Oliver Curtis's redemption tale is riding on it.
Firmus may have hit the big time. Can it last a long time?

Anthropic wants stock market investors to value it at more than $2 trillion. Last year, the company behind Claude AI lost $42 billion.
Both numbers sit in Anthropic’s IPO prospectus, the document a company files before selling shares to the public. Reuters reviewed it on Monday. Here are eight places where the pitch and the numbers pull apart.
Anthropic valuation: $2 trillionAmazon valuation: $2.6 trillionAnthropic revenue: $65 billionAmazon revenue: $716 billionExplain this gap. https://t.co/QPXe39TJp4 pic.twitter.com/ogB8nTsLIo
Anthropic made about $4.6 billion in 2025 revenue. At $2 trillion, investors would be paying roughly $435 for every $1 of sales.
Annualized revenue reached $65 billion in July and could hit $100 billion by year-end. But that is based on the current run rate, not a full year of audited sales.
That makes the valuation unusually dependent on growth continuing almost perfectly. A slowdown would make the $2 trillion figure look much harder to justify.
Anthropic lost $8.06 billion from operations, up from $2.98 billion in 2024.
The headline $42 billion loss exaggerates the cash burn. Yet the underlying business is still losing billions, and those losses are growing.
The business is improving at speed, but it still loses money on every dollar of sales.
Anthropic reported positive adjusted operating income in Q2.
That number excludes model training, partner revenue sharing, and stock-based pay.
This is where the profit claim gets awkward. Training future Claude models is central to Anthropic’s business, yet the adjusted figure removes that cost.
Anthropic spent $7.33 billion on compute in 2025 against $4.6 billion in sales.
That works out to about $1.60 of computing for every $1 of revenue.
For a software company, that is an unusual cost structure. Anthropic must make AI dramatically cheaper to run, or keep raising prices and revenue faster than its infrastructure bill.
Costs bite on the customer side too. Uber burned through its entire 2026 AI budget by April after giving Claude to its engineers. Microsoft restricted internal Claude use over token costs, BeInCrypto reported in May. 
The AI bubble may be hitting reality:-Uber reportedly burned through its entire yearly AI budget in 4 months-Microsoft is cutting internal AI access-Starbucks scrapped an AI system after it performed worse than employeespic.twitter.com/2F3FAhlty6
This is the largest number in the filing. Anthropic plans to spend $518 billion on cloud, computing, and infrastructure obligations in the coming years.
It ended 2025 with $20.28 billion in cash. Many of those commitments reportedly cannot easily be canceled.
The mismatch is huge. Anthropic is locking in hundreds of billions of dollars of spending before anyone knows whether AI demand will stay this strong.
Nearly a quarter of Anthropic’s 2025 revenue came from just two customers. The Reuters report did not name them.
Many major clients also lack long-term contracts.
That makes the growth story more fragile than the headline numbers suggest. Losing even one major customer could materially change the revenue picture.
Government work carries its own risk. A clash with the White House over how its tools are used led the Pentagon to briefly blacklist Anthropic. A US judge blocked that move in August.
Amazon and Google invested billions in Anthropic. They also sell it cloud infrastructure.
That creates an unusual loop: investment money can return to the same companies through cloud spending.
There is nothing inherently improper about that. But it makes it harder to see how much of the AI boom is independent customer demand and how much is tied to Big Tech funding the ecosystem around itself.
Dot-com bubble 🤝 AI bubble""Bro just buy my servers with the money I invested in you.""
Around 80 pages of the filing discuss risks. Just 48 describe the business.
Anthropic says some models resisted shutdown during testing.
“Potential model awareness of our evaluation efforts creates a significant limitation on our ability to assess model safety.”
Michael Burry put it more bluntly:
“IPOs need hype & puffery; ‘we are so awesome it could become dangerous’ is hype & puffery.”
The unusual part is that Anthropic is asking investors to fund rapid expansion while warning that the technology itself may become difficult to control. Few IPOs contain a risk section this central to the product being sold.
The listing is likely to come after the November US midterm elections. That delay would let Anthropic show third-quarter results before it pitches investors on its roadshow.
The benchmark is already on the board. SpaceX listed at a $1.77 trillion valuation on June 12. Its shares closed their first day at $160 and now trade near $147, above the $135 offer price.
OpenAI filed confidentially in June and is expected to list by early 2027. Analysts expect whichever lab lists first to set the price benchmark for the whole AI industry.
Anthropic’s pitch is ultimately a bet that extraordinary growth will arrive before extraordinary costs catch up.

					Lenfest Institute expands AI Collaborative and Fellowship Program with OpenAI support.					Learn more

A project of the Lenfest AI Collaborative and Fellowship Program
By Sonali Verma
September 30, 2026
Share 
The Philadelphia Inquirer publishes hyperlocal newsletters covering specific communities across the Philadelphia area. The team needed a way to reliably surface newsworthy stories from each community from a fragmented ecosystem of sources, such as municipal meetings, school calendars, and Facebook pages. 
One of their editors was spending about 12 hours a week just finding items for newsletters, leaving little time for writing and shaping the voice of the publication. 
Gathering news for these regions was hard because it comes from “a lot of really small sources, and so it’s a lot to sift through,” said Kevin Hoffman, The Lenfest Institute’s AI Fellow who is embedded at The Inquirer.
The Inquirer’s AI tool, Scrape, was created to save time and raise the signal-to-noise ratio of that work.
Scrape worked because it was built around The Inquirer’s workflows, with tight feedback loops from the editor, rather than as a purely technical experiment.
First, the team grounded the tool in the existing manual process. The lead editor had already compiled a curated list of sources and was producing daily tip sheets by hand. Kevin turned that into a direct AI training and evaluation loop.
Their hypothesis was that AI could be used to find newsworthy information. To test it, the team would “run AI every day over the sources that this editor cared about, produce a tip sheet, and then also compare this tip sheet to what this editor produced and understand, ‘Okay, is it actually extracting the same insights? Is it maybe finding new insights that the editor missed, or is it or is it missing in some of those areas?’” Kevin said. 
“And that is exactly what we did over the span of three or four months. It was pretty intense. Every other day, our editor would give me feedback on how Scrape was doing.”
The team would run Scrape and produce an e-mail digest that the editor would annotate, pointing out what was useful and what was not. 
This iterative prompt refinement, where bullet points were added and removed and newsworthiness was defined, gradually shifted the output from noise (such as rescheduled meetings and routine road closures) to genuinely impactful community news, Kevin said.
The second major success factor was embedding editorial context directly into the system. The team used newsletter-specific descriptions, such as the newsletter’s audience, relevant geographical regions, school districts and topics, rather than simply using geography as a guideline for hyperlocal news. In this way, Scrape’s outputs were aligned with what newsletter readers actually cared about.
“For instance, Chester County is one of our newsletters. The editor for the project composed a description for this newsletter that included information on what they were looking for – readers are interested in everything from municipal and local governments to localized state news relevant to them, especially things that will impact their lives, like restaurants and retail businesses,” Kevin said. The prompts explicitly state what not to include, such as straight business news on topics like the stock exchange or personnel changes. They also include information about who the readers are. 
“They are trying to help the models understand what these readers might care about, where their perspectives lie, the relevant communities, and also communities that we actually would not want to include in this newsletter, as well as the relevant school districts,” he said. “We’re passing all this information into Scrape now.”
Editors are pleased with the change, Kevin said. They are now refining the descriptions to produce more actionable tips.
The result: Scrape evolved from a small experiment to “load-bearing and critical infrastructure” for six (and soon, eight) newsletters, with nearly 20 journalists across the newsroom following its output.  The editors’ skills and audience demand has made the local newsletters a success. Scrape is helping the Local team meet demand without overworking the desk. 
One of the hardest problems was generalizing from one editor and geography to many. The original design philosophy was region-based: scrape everything about, say, Lower Merion, then filter using a single “newsworthiness” prompt. That worked “manageably” for one editor and a few newsletters, but did not scale, Kevin said.
Instead of treating “newsworthiness” as a generic, abstract concept, they now anchor it in specific newsletter briefs that editors author themselves – who the readers are, what they’re interested in, and where the coverage line stops.
Another lesson involves cost and search strategy. Early on, the team tried to balance quality with the high cost of searching many small, scattered sources. They discovered that prompting can implicitly control search depth and behavior, but only after trial and error.
In hindsight, Kevin suggests being more explicit, earlier, about acceptable cost-quality trade-offs and designing prompts and architecture around those constraints from day one. 
Scrape’s journey offers a practical blueprint for newsrooms and organizations trying to use AI for finding news in messy local data.
The Lenfest AI Collaborative and Fellowship Program is supported by OpenAI and Microsoft. 
Next						Next

The Lenfest Institute provides free tools and resources for local journalism leaders to develop sustainable strategies to serve their communities.

Google Android

Google announced a new process of sideloading apps on Android back in spring this year, and Android users had mixed feelings about it, to say the least. Google argues that the new rules prevent scammers from pressuring people into installing malicious apps, and four countries are now getting the changes.
Every phone running Android 7 or later in Brazil, Thailand, Singapore and Indonesia now requires users to enable the option in Developer Options, authenticate, restart the device and wait 24 hours before being able to sideload unregistered apps. The setting allows users to sideload apps from unregistered developers and app stores for seven days or indefinitely, depending on what the user chooses.
The authorized app stores for now are, of course, Google Play, Samsung Galaxy Store, Honor App Market, Oppo App Market, Xiaomi GetApps, vivo V-Appstore and Transsion Palm Store.
The new sideload flow is deliberately inconvenient in order to disrupt scammers, but Android users are not convinced, and they believe it may be the first step toward a more closed, iOS-style ecosystem.
For now, only the aforementioned four countries are seeing the new sideload process being introduced, but other markets will start seeing the changes soon enough.

63,2026-10-02,"The smartphone market has plenty of options aimed at seniors in all price ranges. Such phones usually have a simple design and are helpful for older adults who may need features like appointment reminders, navigation while traveling, and immediate emergency contact options. However, if an older person has arthritis or Parkinson's disease, even using a smartphone aimed at seniors might be a frustrating experience. ""They may experience challenges with holding the device, typing, swiping, pressing small buttons, or using pinch-to-zoom features,"" Anna Hicks, M.D., Administrative Director of Age-Friendly Services for AdventHealth in North Carolina, told BGR in an exclusive interview.
The best phones for seniors with Parkinson's disease or arthritis include the RAZ Memory, RAZ SmartVision3, Jitterbug Smart5, Jitterbug Flip 3, and Samsung Galaxy A17. These phones offer important features like large control buttons, which can help seniors with Parkinson's disease press the right button when their hands aren't steady from tremors, Hicks said. They also attempt to help arthritic seniors who have painful finger joints and limited hand strength.
Having phones that offer voice commands, dictation features, the ability to enlarge the icons on the screen, and similar features allows the smartphone to be a helpful tool, according to Hicks. ""People who experience challenges with hand and finger coordination may benefit from a smartphone with a larger screen and accessibility features,"" she said. Hicks suggested that seniors and their families consider their specific needs and potentially speak with their occupational therapists or doctors before picking a phone.
Some seniors who have Parkinson's disease can experience tremors. The tremors ""may make it more difficult to hold a smaller device steady or perform precise movements,"" Hicks said. The RAZ Memory Cell Phone ($369) has a design that simplifies making on-screen selections when hands aren't completely steady. It includes a mode that increases the size of the on-screen buttons, potentially making it easier for someone with mild Parkinson's disease to make an accurate selection. However, because it doesn't use physical keys, it's probably better for someone with arthritis, who may struggle to press physical keys.
The RAZ Memory Cell Phone, which is compatible with all three major cellular networks, received an update for its hand tremor mode late in 2025. The update also gave it improved contrast for people with vision issues and larger keypad buttons, icons, and numbers. People with Parkinson's disease or arthritis will probably want to take advantage of the phone's Auto Answer feature, which means seniors do not have to press a button to answer the phone. Instead, it will automatically answer the call and switch to speaker mode, greatly simplifying things for the user.
Hicks said a smooth glass screen on a smartphone can be challenging to use for some people with Parkinson's disease, as they may struggle with pressing the on-screen buttons. The RAZ SmartVision3 cell phone gives users a tactile keypad that has large numerical buttons and directional buttons to control the screen. The SmartVision3 ($599) is primarily made for people with vision impairments, but many of these same features can benefit seniors with Parkinson's disease. Seniors can switch between using the touchscreen and the navigation buttons, depending on how they're feeling. Arthritis sufferers may struggle with pressing the physical keys on this model.
As an additional helpful feature, users can control the phone through Google Assistant and voice commands instead of the keypad buttons or touchscreen. Just press a button on the side of the phone to activate Google Assistant. You can use Google Assistant for managing calls, dictating text messages, or playing audiobooks. ""People who experience challenges with hand and finger coordination may benefit from ... accessibility features such as voice commands [and] voice-to-text dictation,"" Hicks said.
People suffering from arthritis may experience shaky hands, pain, stiffness, and a reduced range of motion, Hicks said. This can make it challenging for them to press physical buttons on a flip phone. If arthritis sufferers have mild tremors, they might be able to have success using a larger-than-average touchscreen for controlling the phone, like the Jitterbug Smart5 smartphone ($120).
It measures 6.6 inches in height, and its screen stretches to the edges of the phone. An average-sized smartphone has a height between 6.06 and 6.34 inches. Those who have mild Parkinson's disease might also appreciate the larger-than-average device because hand tremors ""may make it more difficult to hold a smaller device steady,"" Hicks said. 
The Jitterbug Smart5 includes access to Google Assistant, giving those with arthritis options for controlling the phone's features via voice commands. It's important to note that Lively was listed as one of the phone carriers to avoid, according to Consumer Reports, because of a below-average customer satisfaction rating. However, if you like the features found with the Smart5, you might not mind using Lively, which is the phone's only compatible cellular service provider.
For those with Parkinson's disease who struggle to hold a thin smartphone, the Jitterbug Flip3's design can be a big help. It has a grippy material on the edges and back of the phone, allowing seniors to feel more confident when holding and using it. ""A secure phone grip, protective case, or adjustable stand may also make the device easier to handle and help prevent drops,"" Hicks said. The Jitterbug Flip3 ($80) is yet again only compatible with the Lively cellular service network.
The Flip3 has a larger body than some other flip-style phones, meaning the buttons are larger and easier to press accurately for someone with Parkinson's disease. However, these buttons may be problematic for someone with arthritis to use. Additionally, the buttons all have backlighting, which can help seniors make accurate selections in low light. Although some people with hand tremors may appreciate being able to fold the phone open or closed to answer or hang up (instead of pressing a button), some people with arthritis may lack the hand strength to complete this action without dropping it. Unfortunately, ""there is no one-size-fits-all solution,"" Hicks said.
Some seniors with Parkinson's disease or arthritis may want a more traditional-looking smartphone that excels in taking photographs. The Samsung Galaxy A17 ($250) includes a 50-megapixel (MP) main camera that features optical image stabilization (IS) technology. Optical IS uses hardware adjustments to balance out any unintended hand movements when shooting photographs that could lead to blurry images. Optical IS is common in cameras for pricier smartphones, so it's a nice option to have it available in a budget smartphone that punches above its price bracket.
Additionally, the Galaxy A17 offers what Samsung calls Easy Mode, which should make it easier to use for people with either Parkinson's disease or arthritis. Easy Mode allows you to enlarge icons and fonts on the screen, making it easier to select the items you want, even if you are suffering from pain or tremors in your hands. You can also customize the settings to change the sensitivity of the touchscreen on the A17, which is helpful for people who may struggle to make accurate touch selections."
64,2026-10-02,"From on-demand platforms like Netflix and Prime Video to platforms with live TV options like Sling TV and YouTube TV, streaming services have risen to become the dominant means of viewing movies, TV shows, and other content. The convenience of streaming services has played a large role in their popularity, but it has also given them significant leverage over customers. Platforms have raised prices significantly over the years and have introduced ads to introductory and even mid-level subscription tiers. While there's still plenty of reason to maintain access to a streaming service, the model does have its drawbacks, and it's worth exploring other ways to watch.
A lot of consumers are ditching streaming for physical media, with 4K Blu-ray being one of the most accessible and popular physical formats available. The convenience of streaming media not only tends to overshadow its pricing, but also its technical limitations. The Blu-ray format can offer superior alternatives to each. If you're looking to expand your content library by way of adding a secondary streaming service to your monthly costs, or if you're currently without a streaming service and looking hard at diving into one, you may first want to take a look at some reasons to start buying 4K Blu-rays instead.
Because streaming services are able to stream in 4K resolution, a lot of subscribers feel they're watching in the highest quality possible. But when it comes to image quality, resolution isn't the end-all measurement. In order to stream across internet bandwidths, compressed bitrates are necessary. Most mainstream services compress a video file down to as little as 8 Mbps, which only contains a fraction of the image quality an uncompressed 4K master file possesses. Audio goes through the same process, with the mix often compressed to around 768 kbps.
Better audio and visuals are one of the reasons every cinephile should own a 4K Blu-ray player. Building a library of 4K Blu-rays in place of a streaming subscription comes with bitrates far superior to what online delivery is capable of. 4K Blu-ray discs typically range between bitrates of 70 Mbps and 100 Mbps, with Blu-ray's capacity maxing out at 128 Mbps. Audio bitrates are also better, especially when paired with various lossless technologies.
The image and audio qualities of a Blu-ray disc also won't ever be limited by a poor internet connection. Because the setup is entirely local, there's no connection between the viewer and the source file that the information needs to be squeezed through. This also keeps one's internet service provider out of the equation and works around any throttling that can take place when streaming lots of movies each month.
One of the biggest problems with streaming services is that movies and TV shows come and go. You can subscribe to a platform in the middle of a month and be faced with an entirely different catalogue just a couple of weeks later. In some cases, the movies that serve as a reason to subscribe can even disappear before you've had a chance to watch them. Another issue is that movies and shows are spread across numerous streaming services, leaving no central place to watch everything.
Purchasing a movie on Blu-ray comes with total ownership in every regard. The movies won't randomly disappear from your shelf each month, nor will they require researching platform libraries to ensure they're available in the first place. 4K Blu-rays are easily purchased through retailers like Amazon, Walmart, and Best Buy, with previously owned titles available at local shops and on eBay. In many cases, the cost of a movie on 4K Blu-ray is similar to a month of subscribing to a streaming service.
Building out a collection of Blu-rays does, of course, require a longer-term mentality. The upfront cost only gets you one movie instead of access to an entire streaming library, but with total ownership comes some other perks. You can watch the movie repeatedly any time you like, and if you ever reach a point when you consider yourself finished with it, you can easily sell it for some cash to purchase a new movie for your 4K Blu-ray library.
Interruption has become one of the worst things about streaming services. Switching to streaming used to mean paying to avoid commercials entirely, but most streaming services now have a paid tier that includes ads, with an even more expensive tier available for those who want to watch without them. Amazon, for example, requires a $4.99-per-month Prime Video Ultra add-on to watch without ads, and Netflix has a similar structure to its subscription tiers, with the removal of all ads requiring a $19.99 per month subscription.
But a 4K Blu-ray tucked away on a shelf is never subject to any advertisements whatsoever. There's no tier to upgrade to when purchasing a movie on Blu-ray, nor can there ever be any kind of algorithm inserting ads based on other Blu-ray movies you've watched. In the same way that owning a Blu-ray disc ensures you'll have access to that particular movie at all times, it also ensures the disc can never be infiltrated and remastered with ads edited in.
Purchasing movies on Blu-ray can also be favorable in other ways regarding ads. Buying movies digitally doesn't quarantine viewers from ads, as these purchases can come with commercials or cross-promotional materials edited into the master download file. Additionally, some smart TVs have been accused of collecting user data, and with ads becoming a potential business model within smart TV operating software, sticking to Blu-rays at least keeps some viewing habits private.
Physical products tend to cost what they cost, with only some sales tax added at checkout. In a general sense, prices do increase over time, but 4K Blu-rays actually cost less than HD Blu-rays did when they first hit the market in the mid-2000s. New 4K Blu-ray releases range from $15 to $25, while the 2006 equivalent releases ranged from $30 to $40. Older movies can cost even less, and anyone willing to sort through the bargain bin at used media shops can find 4K Blu-rays for just a few dollars.
The prices of streaming services, on the other hand, have only gone up. Netflix increased all of its prices early in 2026, and with cost and profit analysis looked at in monthly measurements due to the nature of the subscription model, streaming platforms are constantly looking at ways to generate new revenue. This makes them far more subject to future price increases than the Blu-ray format, and with subscribers coming and going regularly, the pricing, tiers, and add-ons of streaming platforms have the potential to grow more confusing.
There are, of course, plenty of streaming services worth your hard-earned cash. It may even make sense to start purchasing some cheaper Blu-ray movies alongside a monthly subscription to your favorite service. This way, you can start the process of moving away from renting movies on a monthly basis and start moving toward one-time purchase prices and a fully owned movie library.
Streaming a movie only works as well as the connection behind it. In the event of an internet outage or heavy local usage during peak hours, the experience can be severely limited. In some areas, it can be difficult to find stable, consistent high-speed internet access as well. Some streaming services have recommended speeds in order to stream 4K without buffering or signal interruptions.
Netflix, for example, recommends a connection of at least 15 Mbps, as does Prime Video. A poor internet connection can leave a lot of streaming subscribers without access to 4K movies and TV shows at all. Physical media doesn't require any of that. Once a Blu-ray is in your possession, it's as playable as your hardware setup allows for. A Blu-ray player has no need for an internet connection in order to play movies out of a physical library, nor does a smart TV.
All that's needed to put a 4K Blu-ray through its paces is the disc itself, a Blu-ray player, a TV with the proper input connections, and the proper cable to connect the equipment. Leaning on 4K Blu-rays to watch your favorite content also eliminates the consumption of large amounts of monthly data, saving internet users with limited monthly bandwidth plenty of usage for other things."
65,2026-10-02,"South Korean crypto exchange operating profits fell 78% in the first half of 2026 as trading volumes, market capitalization and customer deposits declined.
South Korean crypto exchanges saw operating profits fall 78% in the first half of 2026 as trading activity, market valuations and customer deposits declined, according to new government data. 
On Thursday, the Korea Financial Intelligence Unit (KoFIU) said average daily trading volume at domestic virtual asset exchanges fell 44% from the previous six months, while market capitalization dropped 33% and won-denominated deposits fell 35%. Exchange sales declined 41% over the same period, even as the number of accounts eligible to trade rose slightly by 0.4%.
KoFIU’s survey covered 26 registered virtual asset service providers, including 17 exchange operators and nine custody and wallet providers, and covered activity from Jan. 1 through June 30.
The figures come amid signs that South Korean retail investors have been shifting attention from crypto to the country’s stock market. 
In May, the value of crypto held by South Korean investors fell 50.2% to 60.6 trillion won ($41.4 billion) over roughly a year. Korean outlet ChosunBiz linked the decline to capital moving toward stocks.
A Cointelegraph analysis in July also found that combined average daily volume across Upbit, Bithumb, Coinone, Korbit and Gopax had fallen about 89% year over year during comparable seven-day periods, while the KOSPI, South Korea’s benchmark stock index, had more than doubled over the 12 months to July 22.
Cointelegraph contacted Bithumb and Upbit operator Dunamu for comment.
