All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy."
66,2026-10-02,"It's up to voters whether artificial intelligence will play a role in the outcome of the coming midterm elections.
The AI boom — and the infrastructure needed to power it — has slowly become a flashpoint in US politics. The debate ranges from AI safety, regulation, and deepfakes to the rapid expansion of data centers.
Many Big Tech firms advocate for faster expansion, while local residents have pushed back against data-center projects over their potential environmental and energy-cost impacts.
One big question for voters: What roles should state and federal governments play in regulating AI? Another is how much power local communities should have over the data centers increasingly being built in their backyards.
Governors across the political spectrum — 17 of whom are up for reelection this year — have taken varied approaches to the AI boom. All 39 gubernatorial races, across 36 states and three territories, are on November 3.
Some governors, including California's Gavin Newsom (D) and New York's Kathy Hochul (D), are exploring an AI ""kill switch"" that would disable advanced AI systems in an out-of-control event. Republican Utah Gov. Spencer Cox has also called for federal AI safety rules.
Others, including the Republican governors of Nevada and North Dakota and the Democratic governors of Michigan and New Mexico — whose states have major AI-computing projects under construction — see data centers as an economic opportunity.
Here's what every US governor has said so far about AI, data centers, or both.
Alabama Gov. Kay Ivey (R) has approached AI as an opportunity to improve government, but one that needs guardrails.
In 2024, she created a statewide generative AI task force to examine how it could be used within the state government, emphasizing both the usage of AI and transparency, privacy, and public safety.
""GenAI represents a monumental step forward in the potential for our state government to serve the public,"" Ivey said in a 2024 statement. ""However, its capabilities must first be studied carefully to ensure it is implemented in the most responsible and efficient manner possible.""
She is one of the few governors across the US who have taken a harder line on Chinese AI tools. In 2025, Ivey issued a ban on Chinese-affiliated artificial intelligence platforms DeepSeek and Manus from all state-owned devices and networks.
Alaska Gov. Mike Dunleavy (R) has pitched the state as a potential hub for the AI boom and the computing infrastructure needed for it, arguing that Alaska has an ""arctic advantage.""
Dunleavy has said he believes Alaska's colder climate can significantly reduce the cost of cooling AI server racks, saying in late 2025 that an Alaskan 1-gigawatt campus could save operators up to $150 million annually in cooling costs compared to a warmer state.
Dunleavy has also said that data centers could be an economic driver for Alaska, particularly by creating more construction jobs across the state.
He frequently uses AI, too, incorporating certain tools into his work as governor, including using the technology during meetings.
Katie Hobbs (D), Arizona's governor up for reelection, has largely framed AI as an economic opportunity that the state needs to prepare for, rather than turn away from.
Hobbs has said she does not support a moratorium, which is a temporary pause, on new data-center development. At the same time, she has pushed for more structure around AI.
Her administration created the state's first AI steering committee in 2025 and launched an AI government tool in 2026, and she has described the goal as ensuring AI strengthens rather than undermines the state's economy.
""Arizona has built one of the strongest AI ecosystems in the country, and our community colleges help power it,"" Hobbs wrote in a January 22 post on X.
Arkansas Gov. Sarah Huckabee Sanders (R), who is up for reelection, has said she believes AI has the potential to strengthen government efficiency, but it requires proper policies and regulations.
""AI is changing how we work, interact, and find information — and Arkansas can't afford to be left behind,"" she wrote in a statement in February following a new state AI effectiveness and safety report.
The report, which was completed by an AI working group that Sanders launched in 2024, found that AI could help the state improve Medicaid and SNAP programs and expand other digital services.
Earlier this year, she also joined RAISE US, a national artificial intelligence initiative launched by former Indiana governor Eric Holcomb and former US Secretary of Commerce Gina Raimondo. The initiative intends to partner with governors, employers, workers and training organizations to help the workforce transition to an AI economy.
She was one of several politicians who urged Congress to remove the AI regulatory moratorium from the One, Big, Beautiful Bill in 2025.
California's Gavin Newsom (D) has moved from a more cautious approach to AI regulation toward some of the most aggressive AI-safety proposals in the country.
After vetoing an AI safety bill in 2024, Newsom has since signed legislation creating independent oversight and auditing requirements on AI systems.
Now, Newsom is backing the idea of an AI ""kill switch,"" which would be an emergency shutoff for frontier AI models, as part of a September executive order. New York Gov. Kathy Hochul has proposed a similar idea.
Newsom has also directed experts to consider independent verification of AI companies' safety systems, arguing that California should move ahead even as federal action remains limited.
Colorado Gov. Jared Polis (D) signed Senate Bill 24-205 in 2024, making Colorado the first state to enact a comprehensive consumer-protection law aimed at protecting consumers from AI-driven algorithmic discrimination in areas such as employment, housing, education, and healthcare.
That bill was later repealed by Polis this year, however. In his repeal, he narrowed the state's framework by mandating specific transparency, documentation, and consumer rights when automated tools ""materially influence"" consequential decisions.
The new bill will take effect on January 1, 2027.
Polis has also shown some support for a federal moratorium on state AI laws restricting artificial intelligence, according to a 2025 Bloomberg article.
Ned Lamont (D), governor of Connecticut and up for reelection, signed a bipartisan AI bill earlier this year addressing youth online safety.
Lamont's approach is focused on preparing people to work alongside AI rather than restricting its use. He's also invested in workforce training designed to help Connecticut workers compete for jobs in the tech market: In February, he launched the third phase of the state's Tech Talent Accelerator, a program that aims to expand AI education and workforce training.
Delaware Gov. Matt Meyer (D) has sought to make the state a place where companies can experiment with AI without abandoning regulation.
In 2025, he signed legislation launching an AI regulatory sandbox where companies can test AI technologies under government oversight, particularly in corporate governance, health care, biotechnology and financial services.
Meyer has described the sandbox as a way to combine innovation with transparency and public trust.
""This initiative creates space for visionaries and risk-takers to responsibly test cutting-edge artificial intelligence technologies in Delaware—prioritizing transparency, public trust, and thoughtful oversight,"" he wrote in a statement.
Ron DeSantis (R), Florida's governor, has pushed for protections around AI rather than banning the technology or leaving it unregulated.
His proposed AI Bill of Rights in late 2025 included data-privacy protections, parental controls, consumer disclosures and restrictions on using someone's name, image or likeness without consent.
""The AI Bill of Rights defends consumers, medical patients, and families in Florida from the potential harms of artificial intelligence applications,"" he wrote on X in March. ""It is especially important that we protect the most vulnerable among us: our children.""
Regarding data centers, DeSantis signed a bill earlier this year that would prevent residents from bearing the costs of hyperscale data centers through utility bills and give local governments more authority over where those facilities are built.
Georgia Gov. Brian Kemp (R) sees AI as an economic opportunity the state cannot afford to ignore.
He has backed the expansion of data centers and has positioned Georgia as a potential hub for the country's AI infrastructure, meeting directly with OpenAI and Georgia Power in July to discuss OpenAI's activity in the state, sites of interest, jobs, and investment commitments.
Much of his approach has focused on building the electricity and infrastructure needed to attract AI investment to the state.
Hawaii Gov. Josh Green (D), up for reelection, has taken a cautious approach to AI.
In July, he signed legislation establishing new safety measures for it — particularly surrounding 'deepfakes', which imitate one's voice and face — saying AI presents ""fascinating opportunities"" but that the state needs to be intentional about recognizing its potential dangers.
Idaho Gov. Brad Little (R), who is up for reelection, has focused heavily on preparing students for an AI-driven economy.
In March, his administration launched a partnership with Microsoft, Micron and other technology companies to teach K-12 students and teachers how to adopt AI into the classroom.
While Micron will teach teachers how to use AI, Microsoft will provide AI literacy programs and its chatbot, Copilot, for all students over 13 and teachers.
JB Pritzker, Illinois' governor up for reelection, views artificial intelligence as a transformative tool that also poses severe, existential risks to humanity.
""It's time to sound the alarm — louder — on reining in Artificial Intelligence,"" Pritzker wrote in an early September statement. ""It's becoming more clear the threat AI poses to humanity, so I'm calling for immediate action from the industry and Washington. When AI researchers are whistleblowing, we need to listen.""
His statement urged a stop to Big Tech's lobbying against safety, Congressional hearings on AI safety, and more.
Pritzker also established an AI cabinet in September to assess the various safety risks posed by AI.
Mike Braun (R), Indiana's governor, views AI as an economic opportunity to boost wages, create jobs, and to position Indiana as a leader in AI's adoption.
In April, he launched an initiative titled IN AI to help Indiana businesses understand where AI can improve their work, how to access and use different AI tools, and where to find employees across the state who understand the technology.
He has publicly cautioned against building data centers in the state that drain Indiana's water resources.
The state is home to two large data-center projects in the US, however, housing Meta's data center that is expected to deliver 1 gigawatt of capacity and span 1,500 acres in Lebanon, as well as Google's Fort Wayne Data Center, which will require up to 1.2 gigawatts of power at full build-out.
In February, Iowa's Gov. Kim Reynolds banned DeepSeek and RedNote, two Chinese-owned apps, from state devices. She also prohibited all state agencies from subscribing to or having an account on either app.
Reynolds has shown a focus on how to responsibly adopt AI, signing an Act in May that regulates AI chatbots, particularly for minors. One of the regulations in the Act provides disclaimers at the beginning of each chat and once every three hours.
Kansas Gov. Laura Kelly (D) supports a regulated approach to AI that balances the innovation it brings.
In August, Kelly told the Topeka Capital-Journal that there needs to be guardrails around data center construction and the water and energy use associated with them.
In August 2023, she created an AI policy that requires reviews of accuracy, appropriateness, privacy, and security before any action is taken or information is shared.
Kentucky Gov. Andy Beshear (D) is an advocate for federal oversight and regulation of AI to balance its benefits with risks such as national security and job displacement.
He signed an executive order in August to mitigate the potential environmental and cost impacts of data centers. Specifically, the order requires the companies building the data centers to submit a plan to the state's Energy and Environment Cabinet detailing how they will cover their full energy costs.
Louisiana Gov. Jeff Landry (R) also views AI as a way to drive economic growth, but said it poses security and utility cost risks.
In June, Landry issued an executive order requiring data centers seeking state sales-tax breaks to protect ratepayers from higher electricity costs, unless the state determines the project's other benefits outweigh the higher electricity costs.
In 2025, Landry, like Iowa's governor, also signed an executive order banning China's DeepSeek across all state agencies.
""As technology advances, so do the threats,"" he wrote in a statement. ""This executive order ensures we are protecting our people, our data, and our national security from the CCP's growing influence in AI.""
The state is home to one of the largest planned data centers in the US, housing Meta's Hyperion AI data center, a more than $50 billion investment that will span more than 3,600 acres and is planned to deliver 5 gigawatts of capacity once fully developed.
Maine Gov. Janet Mills (D) has taken a more cautious approach toward the growing AI industry.
This year, Mills vetoed a bipartisan bill that would have placed a temporary pause on data center construction requiring 20 or more megawatts of power. She's also said she supported a federal moratorium if it had exempted a specific project that she believed could bring jobs to Maine.
In June 2025, Mills signed a bill that bans companies from using chatbots without clear disclosure. The bill also added AI-generated deepfakes to the state's revenge porn laws.
Maryland Gov. Wes Moore (D), who is up for reelection, signed an executive order in early September that relieves locals of the costs of the centers' energy usage, includes environmental protection efforts and AI job training, and establishes a ""public dashboard"" where locals can express their input on the centers.
Moore's executive order also established the Maryland Data Center Task Force, which will review data center projects, maintain a public data center dashboard, and more.
""My fear is a technology that no human being can control,"" Moore told MS NOW reporters in late September. ""We've got to understand that delicate balance of cooperation and competition. As a governor, I'm very competitive with my other governors. But I do understand that there are places where the focus is cooperation and not competition.""
Maura Healey (D), Massachusetts' governor up for reelection, has argued that AI shouldn't advance faster than the government can protect people from its risks. In September, Healey called for federal action to strengthen AI guardrails and oversight that both allow for innovation and protect national security.
Like Moore, Healey's administration has also created a state AI task force. The state's task force, created in 2024, also includes a $100 million Applied AI Hub that studies how to apply AI to advance Massachusetts' life sciences, healthcare, financial services, advanced manufacturing, robotics, and education sectors.
""AI has enormous potential to drive scientific discovery, strengthen key industries, improve public services, and create new opportunities for businesses and residents across Massachusetts,"" Gov. Healey wrote in a September press release. ""At the same time, the rapid pace of AI development raises serious concerns about safety, accountability, privacy, civil rights, the future of work, and public trust.""
Michigan Gov. Gretchen Whitmer (D) has welcomed data center construction, such as Oracle and OpenAI's project in Saline Township, but with guidelines.
Her July action plan ensures that data center companies pay construction and operation costs, strengthen and safeguard the state's electric grid and natural resources, and are accountable for their development.
""It's simple: any data center company that wants to invest in Michigan must ensure working families do not pay a single penny for data center development or operations, protect our natural resources, and create local, good-paying jobs,"" Whitmer wrote in a press release.
Michigan is home to The Barn, a $16 billion, 1.4-gigawatt data-center campus in Saline Township that Related Digital is building for Oracle and OpenAI as part of the Stargate project.
Tim Walz (D), Minnesota's governor, signed a law in May prohibiting access to ""nudification"" technology that can generate fake nude images of real people.
""This bill makes clear that using technology to create fake, non-consensual intimate images is unacceptable and puts Minnesota at the forefront of addressing the harms of AI while protecting people from exploitation and abuse,"" Walz wrote in a press release.
The law echoes Walz's broader approach to AI, which encourages its development while drawing lines around any usage that threatens privacy or safety, specifically for children.
In May 2023, he also signed a state law that regulates and restricts the use of deepfakes in political campaigns.
Mississippi Gov. Tate Reeves (R) signed a memorandum of understanding with NVIDIA in June 2025 to promote AI education, workforce integration, and research collaboration.
His focus is on ensuring Mississippi's workforce can use AI effectively; in May, he launched a statewide AI framework that centers on equipping students and workers with skills in ethical reasoning and AI-related cybersecurity awareness.
During a Sept. 30 Federalist Society conversation, Reeves emphasized that Mississippi works efficiently at cutting deals with companies, particularly AI and data center companies, highlighting the state's $20 billion partnership with xAI was expected to launch only a few weeks after making the deal.
""So many states around the country start at no and give you the burden to ultimately get to yes…"" Reeves said. ""…We start at yes, and as we navigate our way through it, we've got very seasoned, talented people who run our economic development shop.""
In May, Mike Kehoe (R), Missouri's governor, announced that Google will invest $15 billion into data center construction in New Florence, a rural town in central Missouri.
Kehoe also signed an executive order in January that allows for growth in data centers and Missouri's use in AI, while calling on economic and natural resources directors to study how AI could impact the local workforce and citizens' energy bills.
The state is home to one of the largest planned data centers in the US, Nebius' AI Factory, which will reach a capacity of up to 1.2 gigawatts.
Greg Gianforte (R), Montana's governor and a former software entrepreneur who founded RightNow Technologies, later acquired by Oracle, has approached AI from a business perspective.
In 2025, he signed Montana's Right to Compute Act, which established legal protections for businesses' use of AI, limiting the government's interference in their use. He also launched 406 JOBS in 2025, a workforce program aimed at modernizing the state's workforce.
Nebraska Gov. Jim Pillen (R), up for reelection, like several other governors, has expressed that locals shouldn't front the bill for energy costs stemming from data centers.
In July, Pillen ended access to certain state tax incentives for data centers and created a task force to examine their impact on water, electricity, and local infrastructure.
""Early in my administration, I called for a pause in building new data centers, and this executive order is consistent with that call,"" Pillen wrote in a press release. ""Data centers are a booming industry. Major tech companies are pouring hundreds of billions of dollars into them. And while that presents significant economic opportunity, it also presents resource challenges.""
Joe Lombardo (R), Nevada's governor up for reelection, has emphasized the economic opportunity AI data centers bring for the state.
In September, he signed an executive order requiring data center developers to cover project-related energy costs. The order also aims to support public schools and protect the state's water resources.
Lombardo has said he supports data centers because they bring jobs, investment, and economic growth to the state.
""At the same time, we have to put the right parameters in place to ensure they don't raise costs on Nevada residents or businesses,"" he wrote in a May Facebook post. ""As Governor, I'm focused on growing our economy responsibly while protecting rates for families.""
New Hampshire Gov. Kelly Ayotte (R), who is up for reelection, has shown skepticism toward data centers due to the amount of energy they require.
In August, Ayotte said she supported a moratorium on data centers, arguing that New Hampshire does not have enough energy capacity to support them without hiking up electricity costs. She also said she is opposed to a potential data center being built in Bow, a small town in central New Hampshire.
In September, New Jersey Gov. Mikie Sherrill (D) issued a $1.07 million fine to the DataOne data center in Vineland, holding that the data center violated the New Jersey Air Pollution Control Act by installing and operating 62 large natural-gas power generators without Department of Environmental Protection permits.
""Data centers will pay their fair share for energy, be accountable for their water and energy use, protect our environment, create good-paying union jobs, and invest in communities,"" Sherrill wrote on a social media post.
She has signed legislation requiring data centers to report their electricity and water consumption to the Board of Public Utilities every six months, and also has expressed that AI instruction should be embedded into New Jersey's education programs.
In 2025, Michelle Lujan Grisham (D), New Mexico's governor, announced a $1 billion partnership with Meta on a new geothermal plant for the company's data center. Geothermal energy, which is considered a green power source, taps heat beneath Earth's surface to generate electricity; it can also be used directly for heating and cooling.
The $1 billion investment will create 3,000 construction jobs and 100 jobs running the geothermal power plant. New Mexico touts 22 data centers — one of which is Project Jupiter, one of the largest in the US.
The state is home to one of the largest planned data centers in the US, Stargate's Project Jupiter, which could include up to 2.45 gigawatts of Bloom Energy fuel cells, replacing an earlier proposal centered on gas turbines and diesel generators.
Kathy Hochul (D), New York's governor up for reelection, like California Gov. Gavin Newsom, is exploring an AI ""kill switch"" that would shut off AI systems in a critical event.
In late December 2025, she signed the RAISE Act, which requires AI developers to create and publish information about their safety protocols and to report incidents to the state government within 72 hours of an incident.
Under the law, New York's Attorney General can bring civil actions against developers who fail to submit reports, with penalties rising up to $1 million for the first violation and up to $3 million for subsequent violations.
Hochul also issued an executive order imposing a one-year moratorium on large-scale AI data centers in July to evaluate and address their environmental and community impact.
North Carolina Gov. Josh Stein (D), like many governors, has approached AI as both an economic opportunity and a policy challenge.
His administration's AI Strategic Roadmap, launched in July, aims to protect locals from any harm AI might cause, prepare workers for AI-related changes, and use AI to improve government work.
In a speech announcing the roadmap, Stein said AI can help with government efficiency, such as scanning outdated documents and answering residents' questions, but also emphasized the need to address the risks that come with its usage.
Kelly Armstrong (R), North Dakota's governor, signed legislation in 2025 that limits the role of AI in certain health-care decisions, requiring licensed physicians, rather than algorithms, to make final decisions in some cases.
Armstrong also sees AI as an economic opportunity, holding that North Dakota's natural-gas industry could help supply the energy data centers need.
Similar to Alaska Gov. Mike Dunleavy, Armstrong believes the state's colder climate can allow AI and data center companies to lower their cooling costs.
Ohio Gov. Mike DeWine (R) said in September that the federal government should enact regulations on AI, not the states.
""Look, as far as AI, this really needs to come from the federal government,"" DeWine said at an event in New Albany, near Columbus. ""We need to have uniformity, not just in this country; we need to have uniformity in the world. So, I would certainly hope we can see some uniformity coming out of Washington.""
In May, he also paused new applications for data center tax exemptions amid growing scrutiny of the state's incentives and the costs associated with large facilities.
According to the governor, in 2025, data centers that had been granted sales and use tax benefits in the state reported a total capital investment of $27.2 billion.
The state is home to one of the largest planned data centers in the US, housing Nvidia-backed project PORTS-Pike Technology Campus, which is expected to include up to 10 gigawatts of data-center capacity.
Oklahoma Gov. Kevin Stitt (R) created an AI task force in 2023 to study how the technology could improve government efficiency, education, and workforce development, and has repeatedly described Oklahoma as a potential AI hub.
Stitt's task force was completed in 2024 and recommended five ways the state can implement AI into its workforce and government, largely by creating oversight, economic, digital, and talent workforce committees that encourage the use of AI across sectors.
""Oklahoma is poised to lead the nation in implementation of artificial intelligence technology, and we have to capitalize on the momentum. Oklahoma truly could be the AI capital of the nation,"" Stitt wrote in a 2024 press release.
Like other governors, he aims to protect ratepayers from increasing energy costs related to data centers, but also has stated that he supports the construction of data centers, highlighting property rights and the free market.
Oregon's Tina Kotek (D), who is up for reelection, has said states cannot wait for federal action on AI.
In September, Kotek issued an executive order similar to those of California Gov. Gavin Newsom and New York Gov. Kathy Hochul, directing the state to assess the viability of a ""kill switch"" requirement for frontier AI models.
""It's encouraging to hear AI companies are beginning to come forward with proposals to address safety concerns, but we cannot simply wait for the private sector to self-regulate a technology that poses such an existential and evolving risk,"" Kotek wrote in a press release.
Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro (D), up for reelection, said at a September AI Horizons Summit that he sees AI's potential to improve productivity and public services, but argued that the technology needs stronger guardrails.
""I want to be clear: I am not a doomer,"" he said at the summit. ""I don't think it is inevitable that AI is going to take over the world or that someone will misuse it to kill us or do harm.""
He also cracked down on data centers in August, saying that data center proposals are scaring and changing the character of Pennsylvania communities.
Rhode Island Gov. Dan McKee (D) created an AI task force and Center of Excellence for AI and Data in 2024, both in an effort to study how AI could increase government efficiency and improve public services.
""Rhode Island recognizes that the rise of AI technology will enhance future government operations and change the way we do business,"" McKee wrote in a press release. ""Rather than simply react to the changes in the information technology landscape, I want Rhode Island to be a leader as this field evolves.""
Beginning in early 2024, Henry McMaster (R), South Carolina's governor, was the chair of the Southern Regional Education Board Commission on Artificial Intelligence in Education.
In his role, McMaster convened with other education and business leaders to chart a course for the use of AI in classrooms and to prepare the workforce for the technology.
""When used correctly, AI is a powerful tool capable of reshaping our education system,"" McMaster wrote. ""By working together, we can overcome the challenges that AI presents and harness its power to ensure our students are prepared for the workforce of the future.""
In general, McMaster is cautious toward rushing into government regulation on AI, arguing that politicians do not have a complete understanding yet of its benefits and drawbacks.
Regarding his view on data centers, McMaster said in late September that he does not plan to call for a statewide data center moratorium.
The state houses three of Google's data centers, which opened in 2007. In 2024, the company announced it would establish two new data center campuses in the state, a combined investment of $3.3 billion.
""Google's latest investment will strengthen South Carolina's position as an emerging leader in technology and innovation,"" McMaster wrote in a statement. 
South Dakota Gov. Larry Rhoden (R), who is up for reelection, has also banned Chinese apps from state devices. 
In 2025, Rhoden signed a bill making it a Class 1 misdemeanor to disseminate an undisclosed deepfake intended to injure a candidate within 90 days of an election, punishable by up to a year in jail, a $2,000 fine, or both.
He is one of several governors who signed a bill that protects residents from increased utility costs caused by data centers.
Bill Lee (R), Tennessee's governor, signed the ELVIS Act — Ensuring Likeness Voice and Image Security Act — in 2024 to protect musicians from being cloned or replicated by AI without consent.
Tennessee was the first state in the US to legally protect artists and musicians from deepfakes. Lee also signed a bill earlier this year that prohibits AI developers from falsely posing as qualified mental health professionals.
The state is home to one of the largest planned data centers in the US, xAI's Colossus 2, which powers Grok, the AI chatbot on X, and is expected to deliver 1.5 gigawatts in capacity.
Texas Gov. Greg Abbott (R), up for reelection, has shifted his views toward AI over the past year.
Less than a year ago, Abbott welcomed the plethora of Big Tech companies constructing data centers across the state, calling Texas the ""epicenter of AI development.""
But with growing backlash toward the uptick in electric and water costs for locals, Abbott has shifted his perspective on data centers, saying that the companies building them ""dug their own grave"" by failing to also build community support.
In August, Abbott announced that OpenAI would comply with his established data center guidelines, including not passing on costs to Texans or interfering with their quality of life.
The state houses several of the US' largest planned data centers, including Meta's El Paso data center and Fermi America's Project Matador, the latter pursuing up to 17 gigawatts of private power.
Utah Gov. Spencer Cox (R) called on Congress in September to establish guidelines on AI, including mandatory reporting of major safety incidents, whistleblower protections for tech employees, independent safety evaluations for models, and restrictions on the sale of advanced AI chips to China.
""We can build the world's most advanced AI here in America while ensuring it remains firmly in service of humanity,"" Cox wrote on X.
In May, he issued an order establishing a ""higher bar"" for future data center projects following public backlash against his support for a project near Salt Lake City. The order requires greater transparency and protections for water, air quality, the Great Salt Lake, local communities, and utility customers.
Vermont Gov. Phil Scott (R), who is up for reelection, is one of many governors who established a task force to assess how AI might shape the state's economy.
In June, Scott's administration partnered with Anthropic through Vermont's Agency of Digital Services to test state IT systems for security vulnerabilities. The agreement gives the state $100,000 in credits for Claude Code, Anthropic's AI tool.
He also signed legislation requiring disclosure of deceptive and fraudelent AI-generated content used in election campaigns within 90 days of an election.
Virginia Gov. Abigail Spanberger is one of several governors who believe states should lead on AI risk assessments rather than wait for Washington to act.
""The federal government needs to play a much larger role in regulating this emerging technology to ensure its safe development and the protection of people and their livelihoods,"" Spanberger wrote in September.
She created a state AI Task Force in September to examine workforce displacement, data privacy, cybersecurity, and ways existing laws can address AI-related harms.
Regarding data centers, Virginia has the largest data market in the US with over 100,000 jobs and over $40 billion in investments, according to the Northern Virginia Technology Council.
Spanberger recently unveiled a Data Center Accountability Framework, requiring centers to pay their own transmission costs, strengthen environmental and water-use protections, and give communities more input on proposed developments.
Bob Ferguson (D), Washington's governor, has expressed concern over children's safety when using AI.
Earlier this year, he signed legislation that requires disclosures that people are interacting with AI, safeguards around self-harm content, and protections for minors from sexually explicit conversations.
Ferguson also signed a bill that requires covered AI providers to include provenance data in AI-generated or materially altered images, video, and audio.
West Virginia Gov. Patrick Morrisey (R) has said he views the state as a ""powerhouse"" for AI and cloud computing, announcing earlier this year that Penzance Management will invest $4 billion in an AI campus in Berkeley County, about 75 miles northwest of Washington, DC.
""By earning the state's first High Impact Intelligence Center designation, Penzance is helping us transform the Mountain State into a powerhouse for artificial intelligence and cloud computing,"" he wrote in a statement. ""This project brings a massive new revenue stream to our communities - all driven by private capital and our commitment to a business-friendly, reliable energy strategy.""
Morrisey has also supported AI health tools that reduce administrative work for rural providers.
Wisconsin Gov. Tony Evers (D) has focused his approach toward AI on how it affects the workforce.
In 2023, he created the Governor's Task Force on Workforce and Artificial Intelligence to assess how the technology could reshape Wisconsin jobs, skills, and key industries. Its 2024 action plan focused on preparing workers and institutions for an AI-driven economy.
He has also welcomed data center projects, saying they can be built while still protecting the environment and the local communities.
Mark Gordon (R), Wyoming's governor, sees AI as an opportunity to grow the state's economy, especially through data centers and advanced computing.
Earlier this year, he issued ""Data Centers the Wyoming Way,"" an executive order intended to create a statewide framework for data-center development, including its energy, water, and community impacts.
""While other states hit the pause button out of political fear, we are choosing action and collaboration,"" Gov. Gordon wrote in a statement to Business Insider. ""We are proving America's digital infrastructure can be built the Wyoming Way.""

The U.S. added 29,000 jobs in September, according to the government’s Nonfarm Payrolls Report released Friday morning. That was below the consensus forecast of 90,000 and compared with August’s gain of 133,000 (revised down from an originally reported 162,000).

In addition to August’s downward revision, July’s 21,000 jobs gain was revised to a jobs loss of 10,000.
Already higher on the session, bitcoin continued just under $87,000 in the minutes following the release. U.S. stock index futures were adding to gains, the Nasdaq rising 1.2%.
The 10-year Treasury yield slumped by 7 basis points to 5.17%, and the 2-year yield fell by a similar margin to 4.71%. Gold gained more than 1%, and the greenback fell versus major currencies.

Supersprint exhaust components have been installed and additional photos of the undercarriage are provided in the gallery.

The Carfax report shows the aforementioned mileage inconsistency, along with a gap in entries between 2012 and 2023.

69,2026-10-02,"Crypto is a sea of green ahead of Friday's U.S. jobs report.
Bitcoin traded above $86,000 at 9:10 UTC, up 3.4% over 24 hours. Ether ETH$2,690.81, XRP (XRP), solana (SOL) and BNB also rose, though none kept pace with bitcoin.
The bigger moves came further down the list. SKY, AAVE and APT jumped 7% to 10%, making them the best performers among the 100 largest coins by market value.
Bitcoin's dominance, or its share of the total crypto market, is closing in on 60%. Meanwhile, the share held by USDT, the largest dollar-pegged stablecoin, slipped to around 6.3%, suggesting traders are moving out of cash and into tokens. These two gauges point to a market growing more comfortable with risk. 
The nonfarm payrolls report, due at 8:30 a.m. ET, is expected to show the U.S. economy added 90,000 jobs in September, down from 162,000 in August. The unemployment rate is forecast to hold at 4.1%, according to FactSet's consensus estimates.
The bigger question for bitcoin is how Treasury yields react, especially inflation-adjusted, or real, yields. Analysts are watching the jobs data and the Oct. 14 consumer price index report for that reason.
""I am watching Friday's payrolls and the 14 October CPI mainly for their effect on longer-dated yields. I use a 10-year real yield of about 3% as a monitoring level, and a sustained move above it would make a retest of $80,000 to $82,000 more likely than a run at $90,000,"" said Oliver Carding, head of marketing at Tesseract Group, which manages $500 million in assets.
Markets now see a 30% chance of a rate hike in October, down from 70%, after dovish remarks from New York Fed President John Williams and Fed Vice Chair Philip Jefferson. Lower odds of a hike tend to support risk assets like bitcoin.
Those odds may not move much unless payrolls come in well above forecasts, according to some observers. A big upside surprise could revive hike bets and potentially put pressure on bitcoin.

74,2026-10-02,"October 2, 2026
Alexis Nikole Nelson, aka Black Forager, reveals the wild foods hiding outside your door

How foraging can change the way we think about food and nature
By Rachel Feltman, Fonda Mwangi, Alex Sugiura & Alli Rodgers 
S&S/Simon Element/Scientific American Illustrations
Rachel Feltman: For Scientific American’s Science Quickly, I’m Rachel Feltman. And normally I’d tell you who today’s guest is but she described herself so delightfully that I want you to hear it in her own words:
Alexis Nikole Nelson: Alexis Nikole Nelson, field botanist, eater of things off ground?
Feltman: Even if you don’t know Alexis by name, you’ve almost certainly seen some of her work on social media as Black Forager. Millions of followers devour her videos about finding and eating wild foods, from fruits to flowers to seaweeds. She’s here today to talk about her debut book, Happy Snacking, Don’t Die!
If you're enjoying this article, consider supporting our award-winning journalism by subscribing. By purchasing a subscription you are helping to ensure the future of impactful stories about the discoveries and ideas shaping our world today.
Thank you so much for coming on to chat with us today.
Nelson: Oh, my gosh, I’m so happy to be joining you.
Feltman: So let’s talk about your new book a little bit and how you got to be writing it. I would love to just go back in time a little bit and hear how you first got into foraging.
Nelson: So I began foraging at, like, the ripe old age of five with my mom. I used to love tagging along when she was working in the garden on the weekends. And I don’t know if you know this, famously, five-year-olds, not the most helpful genre of human. Motor skills not fully downloaded yet. And so whether it was an act of love and sharing knowledge or an act of trying to keep a small child busy, my mom pointed out some onion grass that was growing in our back lawn, and I had never noticed it before.
I asked her when she put it there. She said she didn’t, it was just there on accident, and that there were lots of plants that were just there minding their own business that were also edible and medicinal. And to me, a small child, that blew my mind. I was like, “What do you mean you didn’t put it here? And why isn’t everyone talking about this? Alert the presses. Alert the masses.” And that just sparked my love of knowing all things edible that happened to be growing in my general vicinity, which, you know, has now spread to just a love of the natural world in general.
Feltman: You know, speaking of the love of the natural world and, you know, how foraging feeds that for you, tell me about, you know, what you love about foraging and why it feels so important to share with other people.
Nelson: I think it’s two main things that I really love about it. One is: It’s really meditative. It’s a beautiful way to force your brain to slow down and to notice tiny details about the world around you, to participate in your local ecosystem. And I think that does my brain a whole world of good. I know I’ve been traveling a lot lately, so I haven’t been able to, and I can definitely tell the difference in brain when I am outside in the woods every day and brain when I’m not.
And additionally, I think especially this past summer, we have recognized how fragile some of our food systems are and how maybe we have created such a big food system in which we’re so separated from the food that ends up on our plates that maybe we’ve taken a bridge too far. So it’s really nice to have some things in your pantry, on your dinner plate, that you know the entire trajectory of its travel from being in the ground to being on your plate.
I think it’s a good skill to have. I don’t think it’s ever gonna replace the grocery store for anybody, but there’s something really fulfilling about being responsible for some of the things you’re eating. You know, people who have hobby vegetable gardens will say the exact same thing.
Feltman: So in doing this work publicly including on the Internet, what kinds of questions do you get from the people who are watching what you’re creating?
Have you been surprised at all by what kinds of feelings come up in people when they, they see you sharing stuff about foraging?
Nelson: Yeah, absolutely. I think the two most surprising questions that I still get with some amount of frequency is always a question of where I’m foraging and the legality of me as an individual foraging there.
Feltman: Interesting.
Nelson: And there’s a lot to unpack in that particular question, ’cause sometimes it is really coming from a place of, like, earnest curiosity. A lot of times, some people are also kind of telling on themselves with some of their own inherent biases, and I try to talk through that with people to the best of my ability, ’cause a lot of our feelings about trespass and who has access to wild spaces ends up becoming, like, a classism conversation, I think, a lot faster than people necessarily expect it to.
And I also don’t think a lot of people realize that the origin of a lot of trespass law lies in purposefully cutting off poor people and people of color from being able to access wild foods, be they plants, fungi, fish, squirrels, on land that doesn’t belong to them.
The other question very much in that same vein is “Well, what happens if everyone starts foraging? What’s gonna be left?” People think everyone’s gonna see one of my videos, and then it’s just going to be, like, a cartoon desert with a tumbleweed going by—except maybe no tumbleweed, ’cause, you know, that’s a brassica. Somebody might try to eat that, too. And the first thing that I love to assure people is everyone’s not gonna become a forager.
I know, ’cause I try. I can’t even get my whole family to go foraging with me. And that’s okay. Choose your own adventure, like the books at the library in the ’90s. Choose your own adventure. I am not here to yuck anybody’s yum, and sometimes people are city mice and inside mice, not country mice, and that’s fine.
Second of all, I think, to assume some sort of, like, capitalist intent behind every single person who is foraging is also kind of telling on yourself if that’s the frame of mind you’re going into asking the question with—because I do honestly and earnestly believe that if people were just foraging for the sake of being able to take care of themselves, their family and the community in which they live, and that includes your human neighbors and your not human neighbors, too, then they will make an informed choice while they are gathering.
You know, there are a lot of different global cultures that have rules and rituals to follow, whether that is, you know, some of the Indigenous bands here in the United States taking a moment to pause, lay down tobacco, because that moment of mindfulness helps you from going overboard. You know, globally there’s a lot of also, you know, never take the first thing that you see of a specific species. You know, never take a noticeable amount of a specific species—because not only would that be bad for the thing you’re gathering, but in the long run, it’s also bad for you.
Feltman: Right.
Nelson: You know, foragers are constantly having to be future-focused. We are constantly in a conservationist mindset, not just because we also want to keep having that experience, but we would love people a generation down the road, seven generations down the road, to also get to foster these relationships with our nonhuman neighbors, with the trees and the plants and the fungi and the animals that we happen to be lucky enough to live near.
I think foraging becomes poaching when you start putting money into the equation. I live in a part of the country that is, you know, in the ramp belt. And if you’re not a foodie, a ramp is a very pungent wild onion that is in season for about a month in the Midwest.
Feltman: And people lose their minds.
Nelson: They lose their minds. People lose their ever plant-loving minds over them.
Martha Stewart wrote one article about them two decades ago, and the ramps have not recovered since. And you will see people, in order to be able to stock, like, farmers markets in Manhattan and in order to be able to stock, you know, the kitchens of Michelin-starred restaurants around the country, coming into places like southeast Ohio, coming into West Virginia, and filling up trash bags with this plant that frequently can take nearly a decade to reach maturity. You know, [it] can sometimes take 10 years before it is at a point where it can be procreating, creating seeds. Those seeds take an additional two years then to break down their seed coat and germinate. So you, you do have to be using your noggin when you’re going out and gathering.
And if you’re just gathering enough for you and your family to have a mess of greens for a couple nights in the spring and to pickle a couple jars of bulbs, that’s totally fine. I’m not the person who’s gonna be, like, pick-shaming people for going and gathering ramps. They’re a wonderful springtime treat. But yeah, the second that you put money into the equation and start putting more steps in between the gathering and the consuming of the plant, the muddier the waters become, the less careful the people doing the gathering suddenly start to be because you have this magical dollar sign hovering over your head that you’re chasing.
Feltman: Yeah. So let’s talk about your book. Who is it for, and what are they going to come away with after reading it?
Nelson: My book is for current and future foragers. I think people assume you have to be at my level to be considered a forager, and I think if you just go and dig up the dandelions in your backyard, congratulations, you are a forager. Your laminated name tag will be in the mail shortly, I promise. And I want this book to help people recognize that and also help them recognize that you don’t have to live in the middle of the woods on an expansive piece of land in order to become a forager.
There are so many opportunities. I live in the middle of a city. I love getting—you know, another question I get a lot is “Oh, where do you live where you find all of the”—Columbus, Ohio. We often don’t take stock of the plants that do exist around us. Even if you feel like you’re living in a concrete jungle, odds purslane is still coming up out of the sidewalk cracks. There you go. That’s a snack that you could be foraging. Make sure it’s not in a place where a dog could have peed on it, but you know, that’s still something forageable in the heat of summer, surrounded by nothing but industry and concrete.
Feltman: Yeah, very cool. What are some other of your favorite sort of like gateway-drug foraging things that, like, are more abundant than people realize, that they’re gonna love once they try them, and then they won’t be able to stop picking stuff?
Nelson: Ooh, my favorite is actually a throwback for both of us, Rachel, and that is serviceberries, which secretly have a million names. If I don’t call them by the name that you at home specifically call them, I’m so sorry, but we don’t have time....
Feltman: And you can go listen to my other show,The Weirdest Thing I Learned This Week, if you want to hear, still probably not literally every name for the serviceberry, but many more of them.
Nelson: They’re such a popular planting for parks and lining city streets because they’re resilient, their foliage is beautiful in the autumn, and their fruits are so good. Amelanchier arborea is the species that I see used in parks globally the most. I saw an Amelanchier arborea in the middle of Tokyo earlier this year.
So when I say that there is one hiding near you, that is not a joke. Unless you live right around the equator, where it’s a little too toasty for them, you will probably find a park close to you that has these guys, and they are such a springtime treat. You might have to fight some birds for them. I have an uneasy truce with the robins in my neighborhood. They’re like, “You don’t bring out the berry rake, we don’t poop on you. That’s the deal.”
Feltman: Well, and so as an experienced forager, I would also love to hear, to inspire our guests for their future foraging endeavors: What are some of the more surprising or challenging things that you have foraged yourself?
Nelson: Ooh. Well, I was in Glacier National Park last summer and just hanging out on a mountainside, eating alpine strawberries. That was fun and worth it. I mean, that hike did kick my whole butt. But also, if you’ve never had a wild strawberry, I need you to pencil it in for next summer because the amount of flavor that gets packed into one of those little guys is insane. They’re so good.
Feltman: Well, another one that I would love to hear more about, I think you recently did some ocean pickling, uh, which of course, so much interesting science there. So for our listeners who don’t follow you yet online, it would be great if you could tell us a little bit about that one.
Nelson: So I love food history, especially when it comes to things like foraging and fermentation and older food preservation methods. And there is a pretty strong bit of evidence for humans discovering lacto-fermented pickles through preserving things in saltwater, because frequently, so long as it hasn’t rained super recently, ocean water is right around the salinity that you want to make a good sour lacto-fermented pickle—like, high 2 percent to high 3 percent range, enough that you get some good salty flavor but also not so salty that the lactobacteria can’t get really active and create that delicious lactic acid that we love, that wonderful crisp acidity that we love from a good pickle.
So to showcase this, while I was visiting some of my family on Martha’s Vineyard—far away from industry, I promise, that’s always everyone’s biggest fear—I checked the water quality levels before I did this. I gathered up a bunch of seawater, put it through multiple layers of filtration. We filtered out the bits, big and small, brought it up to a rolling boil for just a moment, just to kill off anything too small to be able to be filtered out.
And then I have pickled beets, jalapeños, garlic. They all pickle beautifully. And due to the fact that our ocean water, it’s not just sodium making a salt in the ocean, we also have some magnesium salts in there, too, which I think add to the flavor profile of the pickles. They are pretty slammin’, if I may say so myself. And I love an excuse to sneakily shove some science education into my videos.
Feltman: Amazing. Well, thank you so much for coming on to chat with us. This has been wonderful.
Nelson: Thank you so much for having me.
Feltman: That’s all for today’s episode. We’ll be back on Monday with our usual science news roundup.

A yellow wristwatch is not that uncommon, especially for brands that love playing around with bright colors. But to see a watch that pays tribute to bananas? Now that’s something you don’t see every day. Bananas aren’t my favorite fruit, but I know a lot of people who love them and the color most closely associated with them. This latest collaboration between G-SHOCK and BAIT will appeal to that particular crowd.
The BAIT x G-SHOCK DW5600 “Yamagata’s Finest” turns the watch brand’s familiar square digital watch into a ripe banana on your wrist. Not only is its resin case yellow, but the design also carries the brown “freckles” that appear when a banana is ripe for eating. BAIT has built a whole world around this unusual watch, too, which collectors might enjoy as much as the watch itself.
Designers: BAIT x G-SHOCK













The ripe-banana motif connects to what a watch is supposed to do. Both the brown freckles and the watch tell you, in their own ways, “It’s time.” Sure, it’s a visual joke, but it’s also a fun way to make a watch that can start a conversation with someone wondering why you’re wearing a banana on your wrist. The name is a tribute to Japan’s Yamagata Prefecture, which is associated with G-SHOCK production and, perhaps more surprisingly, greenhouse-grown bananas. That local connection gives the design a story beyond its bright color.
And it’s not just a plain banana-yellow watch. Aside from the freckles, there are brown accents around the bezel and on the strap, including a darkened strap tip that resembles the end of a banana. BAIT even created a banana mascot named “Shawty with Freckles” and engraved it on the stainless-steel caseback. You’ll also see the mascot when you activate the electroluminescent backlight. The packaging keeps with the theme: the watch comes in a yellow tin labeled “BAIT Farms Non-GMO.” It’s a playful detail, not a claim about the watch’s materials.



Beneath all that personality, this is still a practical DW5600. The watch measures about 42.8 mm across and 13.4 mm thick, with a resin case and strap. It offers 200-meter water resistance, along with a stopwatch, countdown timer, alarm, calendar, and backlight. Those familiar features mean the banana theme doesn’t come at the expense of everyday usefulness. You can check the time at a glance, time an activity, or set an alarm, then get on with your day. It’s a novelty in appearance, but it doesn’t have to be treated like one when you wear it.
That balance is probably the biggest benefit for anyone considering it. Some statement accessories look great but don’t do much else; this one adds a sense of humor to a watch with useful functions. The yellow color makes it easy to spot in an outfit, while the smaller details give people something to notice up close. It could be a fun choice for someone who already likes G-SHOCK’s square shape but wants a version that feels less ordinary. And for a banana enthusiast, the mascot and tin make the whole package feel more like a gift than a standard watch purchase.



The limited-edition watch was priced at $170 and launched through BAIT stores and BAITMe.com at the end of September 2026. Availability may vary now, so anyone hoping to pick one up should check current listings rather than assume it is still in stock. For collectors, the themed packaging may be part of the appeal; for everyone else, the main question is whether the joke makes them smile enough to wear it regularly.
I don’t have to love bananas to appreciate how thoroughly BAIT and G-SHOCK committed to the idea. The freckles, darkened tip, mascot, backlight, and tin all work together, while the familiar DW5600 keeps the watch useful. It won’t suit anyone looking for a subtle accessory. But if you like your everyday gear with a little personality, “Yamagata’s Finest” has arrived at just the right time.

78,2026-10-02,"When looking back at AMD GPUs released between 2020 and 2025, some of these cards span several generations of underlying architecture and require older drivers to work properly. But if you're looking to save a few bucks, you're likely wondering whether an old AMD card is worth it, given the memory and RAM shortage driving up component prices to ridiculous amounts. But the feature loss, pricing, and hardware/software compatibility issues with a badly outdated graphics card just aren't worth going back half a decade for.
In 2020, AMD released the Radeon RX 6800 and Radeon RX 6800 XT, along with the AMD Radeon RX 6900 XT. These were based off of the company's RDNA 2 architecture, also used in Sony's PlayStation 5, Microsoft's Xbox Series S/X, and Valve's Steam Deck. Pricing is a bit inconsistent, and will vary by seller and brand. You'll also likely be forced into buying more experience variants of the cards, which came with better fans and features, but didn't sell as well after the launch fervor. Expect to spend about $800 for the RX 6800 XT (down about 3% from adjusted launch MSRP), $630 for the RX 6800 (about 12% down from launch), and $999 for the RX 6900 XT (down nearly 17% from its MSRP). Note that you can only acquire them new through third-party distribution from online retailers, which is another concern. By comparison, the Radeon RX 9070 XT costs around $830 (38% markup), but is also much more modern since it is based on the new RDNA 4 architecture that's half a decade newer than the RX 6000–and it's AMD's strongest graphics card available in 2026.
You can, however, get older AMD Radeon cards for cheaper on the used market. But you won't know how much of a beating they've taken in someone's computer, or whether they have any hard-to-spot damage or degradation. Plus, there are no manufacturer warranties to protect you, and the listing you find could end up being a scam for what you're paying–so it's much better to buy from retail if you're lucky enough to spot one.
If, circumstantially, your computer breaks down and you desperately need an AMD GPU replacement, downgrading might be the viable option if you can't find a new one in stock. But going back beyond products launched in 2024 generally isn't worth it. Even if those old cards eventually get newer features, they don't show up as quickly as they do on modern GPUs. That means you'll have to wait longer for free upgrades like FSR 4, if you'll get it.
Some newer graphically-intensive games also aren't optimized for older cards. For example, one of AMD's 2023-2024 budget cards is the Radeon RX 7600, which is $400 for one of the cards from XFX. This AMD chip is marketed as designed to play games at 1080p, and you'll start noticing technical hiccups with its 8GB VRAM at higher resolutions with maxed out textures. Still, if you're mainly a light gamer, a 48% bump from its original price isn't the worst in the world if it's maxing out your budget.
Its premium sibling is a lot more in line with what you'd expect from newer AMD cards today. The AMD Radeon RX 7600 XT, comes at $695 (over 111% markup from the $329 MSRP), which has double the VRAM, can support native 1440p, and provides a higher clock speed. The problem is that if you're trying to push heavier ray tracing workloads at higher resolutions, you might run into some inconsistencies with performance–that's when you start noticing a drop in value compared to current cards. At that point, you should just snag the AMD Radeon RX 9070 XT on sale instead, as that gets you the best GPU AMD currently has on the market, and it's about equivalent in performance to Nvidia's GeForce RTX 5070 Ti."

80,2026-10-02,"Google Pixel was designed to be a premium smartphone effort, but that all changed in 2019 with the debut of the Pixel 3a. The budget-conscious launch went on to be a turning point for the Pixel’s history and, looking back, it might just be the single most important Google smartphone ever.
Looking back over the past decade of Pixel smartphones, I’m not sure you can point at any one device and say it was more important than the Pixel 3a. The original Pixel set the foundation, of course, and Pixel 6 launched Google’s efforts into a new era, but Pixel 3a gave Google a taste of what it was always after – market success.
The Pixel 3a almost immediately shot up to the top of best-seller lists, massively propelled Google’s US smartphone sales, and was the driving force behind setting Google’s first big shipment records. That success ultimately led to several Pixel A-series successors that have similarly resonated in the market. These more affordable devices just nail the fundamentals at a price that’s hard to beat, especially considering the cost of a Pixel 10a is only $100 more (annnnnnd now it’s $200) than the Pixel 3a originally started at.
Google is finding at least some success in the flagship market nowadays. Pixel phones are very much hitting their stride in 2026 not only in terms of design and functionality, but also in the market as a whole. I’d very much argue, though, that the Pixel 3a was the lifejacket that kept this project going. The failure of the Pixel 4 left scars on Google’s smartphone efforts, but the Pixel 3a’s resounding success was surely a huge deciding factor on what would come next.
And, in 2026, I think this phone holds up remarkably well from a hardware perspective. There are modern budget phones that cost just as much as the Pixel 3a did when it came out that feel a lot worse than this one. The Pixel 3a is long dead at this point with software support having ended in 2022, but its legacy lives on.
